Those details come from LawSites, Bob Ambrogi, 2026-05-14 (https://www.lawnext.com/2026/05/netdocuments-unveils-legal-context-graph-to-map-legal-knowledge-alongside-a-reimagined-platform.html), which says 'select customers on the company's enterprise AI tier' and public preview 'expected in the coming months'; Legal IT Insider also says 'Enterprise AI tier'. Cite them to the trade press, not to NetDocuments. Press release dated 2026-05-14 confirmed: title; 'This is the deepest piece of platform engineering we have ever shipped, and it is the one that matters most.'; 'You cannot retrofit this. It has to be built into the core.' (Dan Hauck, CPO); AWS and Elastic; scope list; no mention of spend, e-billing, outside counsel or matter management. iManage 'context fabric' at ConnectLive 2026 in Chicago in May confirmed via LawSites (2026-05-22). The 'document-centric is one layer' point remains the researcher's inference.
13. **Legal trade press notes DMS vendors converging on 'context' and flags an unresolved governance gap: auditing exactly which documents and context fed an AI output.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; trade-press; 2026-05-14]
   Neil Cameron, lead analyst, Legal IT Insider, observes NetDocuments and iManage reached the same conclusion within weeks, calls auditability 'a live issue for the whole industry,' and says the DMS 'sits in a privileged position to be the trust-and-governance substrate for legal AI.' LawSites (Bob Ambrogi, 2026-05-14 and 2026-05-22) reported both launches without critique.
   Quote: "AI in legal work is only as useful as the context it can reach"
   https://legaltechnology.com/netdocuments-reimagines-the-dms-around-context/
14. **Existing legal standards are classification vocabularies and exchange formats, not operational ontologies: SALI LMSS (OWL, 18,000+ tags), LEDES/UTBMS (e-billing formats and task codes), FIBO (OWL finance ontology, OMG-standardised), Akoma Ntoso and LegalRuleML (OASIS standards for legal texts and norms).** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; primary; unknown]
   SALI's repo calls LMSS.owl 'SALI's primary taxonomy/ontology' with unique IRIs per tag; v3 is being released attribute by attribute. LEDES Oversight Committee lists 98B, 98BI, 2000, XML Ebilling 2.0-2.2, Budgeting, Timekeeper Attributes, IPMM, API v1 (2020) and UTBMS code sets. LegalRuleML Core v1.0 became an OASIS Standard on 2021-08-30. FIBO 'defines the sets of things that are of interest in financial business applications.' A legal operations ontology would reference these as property values and interchange formats rather than adopt them as its schema (my framing).
   Quote: "SALI's primary taxonomy/ontology"
   https://github.com/sali-legal/LMSS
15. **Independent observers rate Fabric IQ as consequential but caution that building and maintaining an ontology is a major organisational effort that tooling does not solve.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; trade-press; 2025-12-02]
   Barry Briggs (CTO, Directions on Microsoft, 2025-12-02): 'Of all the announcements at Ignite, the IQs will become the most important and consequential,' but 'Few enterprises have a well-architected enterprise data model'; recommends starting at department level; compares with the more mature but consulting-heavy Palantir Foundry. Nikola Ilic (Data Mozart, 2026-02-03): 'If your organization struggles to agree on basic definitions now, an ontology won't magically create consensus.' Juan Sequeda (ServiceNow, 2026-06-10): 'Start small and think big.'
   Quote: "Creating an ontology, in Fabric or elsewhere, is a major effort and requires on-going maintenance."
   https://www.directionsonmicrosoft.com/cio-talk-microsoft-gets-iq/
   FACT-CHECK: Barry Briggs is an Analyst at Directions on Microsoft, not its CTO; his bio says he was formerly CTO of Microsoft's own IT organisation. The headline quote is a truncated sentence; the original reads 'Creating an ontology, in Fabric or elsewhere, is a major effort and requires on-going maintenance, so there's no getting out of the hard work.' The 'most important and consequential' line is prefaced 'My personal opinion is that'. Directions on Microsoft article datePublished 2025-12-02; all Briggs quotes verbatim, plus the department-level recommendation and the Palantir comparison (forward-deployed engineers or Accenture/Deloitte, 'expensive and time-consuming'). Nikola Ilic quote verbatim, dated 2026-02-03 (https://data-mozart.com/beyond-the-lakehouse-first-thoughts-on-fabric-iq/). Juan Sequeda 'start small and think big' verbatim (lower-case, mid-sentence), 2026-06-10 (https://juansequeda.substack.com/p/servicenow-is-joining-open-semantic).

Viewpoints:
- Platform vendors (Microsoft, Palantir, ServiceNow, SAP): the ontology is the shared business-context layer that lets multiple agents reason consistently and take governed action; Palantir goes furthest, saying the ontology models decisions (data, logic, action, security), not just data.
- Gartner: semantics and a context layer are now critical infrastructure for agentic AI; a single universal semantic layer is elusive so leaders are moving to 'composite' semantic layers; business decisions should be explicitly modeled and governed (decision intelligence, decision governance).
- Investors: Foundation Capital says the prize is a system of record for decisions built from decision traces captured in the execution path; a16z says agents are a context problem and the context layer must be a superset of the BI semantic layer.
- Knowledge-graph practitioners (Jessica Talisman, Gartner's Afraz Jaffri, George Anadiotis, Juan Sequeda, Kurt Cagle): 'context graph' is largely a rebrand of ontology and knowledge-graph work; decision traces without a formal ontology are just logs; start small rather than modeling the whole enterprise.
- Pragmatic sceptics of Fabric IQ (Barry Briggs, Nikola Ilic): consequential but early and still preview; ontology is a major ongoing effort; tools do not create organisational agreement on definitions; capacity cost and technical limitations are real.
- Legal DMS vendors (NetDocuments, iManage): the DMS is the natural home of legal context because work product, permissions and ethical walls live there; context must be built into the core, not retrofitted.
- Legal trade press (Neil Cameron, Legal IT Insider): convergence on 'context' is real and may squeeze horizontal AI vendors, but per-prompt auditability of what context fed an AI output is unproven.
- Interoperability camp (Snowflake-led Open Semantic Interchange with Salesforce, dbt Labs, ServiceNow and others): solve semantics with an open, vendor-neutral semantic-model interchange standard rather than one vendor's ontology; notably the OSI announcement never uses the word 'ontology.'

Gaps:
- The session's web search budget was exhausted after 19 searches in this track, so planned searches on Databricks, Salesforce, Forrester and legal-vendor knowledge graphs could not be run.
- Gartner primary pages (press releases of 2026-05-11 and 2026-06-16, the decision intelligence glossary, and research notes) returned HTTP 403; Gartner quotes and predictions were read from mirrors (IT-Online, MarketScreener) or an Atlan-published newsletter and must be verified against gartner.com. Gartner's own definition of decision intelligence was not verified; only a vendor paraphrase (ACTICO) was found.
- Microsoft Fabric blog and community posts (Ignite November 2025 announcement, FabCon March 2026 'What's next for Fabric IQ Ontology', June 2026 'shared context layer') returned 403. The line that a semantic model describes how data is stored while an ontology describes what the business is comes from a search snippet only and should not be quoted without re-verification.
- No Microsoft source was found announcing general availability of the ontology item as of 2026-09-21; the Build blog says Fabric IQ is GA while Microsoft Learn still labels the workload 'IQ (preview)', an inconsistency the article should word around.
- Exact publication day of the Foundation Capital essay: secondary sources say 2025-12-22; the fetched page exposed a 2026-09-17 timestamp that is probably a modified date. The $4.6 trillion framing was seen only in search snippets.
- No published critique was found that explicitly argues legal 'context graphs' are document-centric and therefore only one layer; that observation is my inference from the scope of NetDocuments' own materials.
- No evidence found of ELM or matter-management vendors (Onit, Mitratech, Brightflag, SimpleLegal), Thomson Reuters, LexisNexis, Harvey or Legora publishing a legal ontology or knowledge graph, nor of CLOC or ACC publishing a legal operations data model. This is absence of evidence only.
- Forrester: nothing retrieved. Databricks and Salesforce: no direct 2025-2026 statement on ontology retrieved; Salesforce appears only as an OSI participant. SAP Knowledge Graph details are from search snippets, not a fetched page.
- SALI LMSS v3 release status and date not pinned down; FIBO 2026/Q2 class count (2,442) and a reported mid-2026 Akoma Ntoso revision come from search snippets only.
- iManage general availability timing is conflicting: LawSites says no GA date was announced, while a search snippet of iManage's own release says October 2026.
- The Sequeda benchmark (16% to 54% accuracy) dates from November 2023 on GPT-4; it is directional evidence, not a current figure. No 2025-2026 benchmark of ontology-grounded agents was retrieved.
- Several quotes were returned with ellipses or as near-paraphrases (NetDocuments CEO Josh Baxter, a16z 'superset' sentence, Talisman 'rebranding', Anadiotis 'Logic Gap'); these are flagged in the notes file and need re-verification before use as direct quotes.

Fact-checker overall: All 15 findings hold up in substance: 10 confirmed, 5 corrected on details, none refuted. Corrections that matter for publication: (1) Barry Briggs is an Analyst at Directions on Microsoft, not its CTO (he is a former CTO of Microsoft IT). (2) The Gartner 80%/60% semantics prediction should be attributed to a Gartner press release of 11 May 2026; the 'said at the London summit' detail was not seen anywhere. (3) NetDocuments' three levels, 'model-agnostic' and 'enterprise AI tier' come from LawSites and Legal IT Insider, not the press release. (4) The 'Ontologies can be generated directly from semantic models...' quote is on the Fabric IQ overview page, not concepts-generate. (5) The Year of the Graph DEV.to copy is dated 2026-04-18; the original is 2026-03-19, and Blumauer's wording is 'adding time and decision lineage'. Upgrades: the Foundation Capital essay date is now confirmed as 2025-12-22 from the page's own time element (authors Jaya Gupta and Ashu Garg); the Jaffri quote is verbatim from his own LinkedIn post (~2026-01-08) and Talisman's 'meaning isn't the same as measurement' is verbatim in her 2026-01-22 Metadata Weekly piece, so both can be cited to primaries. Remaining gaps: Gartner.com blocked every request (403), so both Gartner items rest on mirrors; '17 vendors' and 'first' for the Decision Intelligence Platforms Magic Quadrant were not seen; and because the session's WebSearch budget was exhausted and public search engines returned CAPTCHA/429, I could not search for a post-June Microsoft announcement of ontology GA - the 'still preview' status rests on Learn docs updated 2026-09-11. Microsoft's own materials are inconsistent (blog says Fabric IQ is GA; docs still say 'IQ (preview) workload'). Two quotes are mid-sentence in their originals (a16z, Sequeda) and one is the analyst paraphrasing a vendor (Legal IT Insider). Verification notes written to C:/Users/RALPHS~1/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--code-files-spaarke-website/aa6b682a-4844-485c-99e0-cb2228b928c3/scratchpad/research/ontology-landscape-and-ai.verified.md. No repository files were modified.


## research:changing-role-of-legal-ops

Evidence from late 2025 through September 2026 supports the thesis, with important caveats. CLOC/Harbor data (135 large departments) shows demand rising while budget and headcount flatten; only 37% expect more outside counsel spend (58% a year earlier), 85% have an AI governance resource or committee, 61% run convergence programs and 65% deliberately keep work in-house. ACC's 2026 CLO Survey (1,049 CLOs) shows a record 84% reporting to the CEO and 47% told by CEOs to build AI proficiency, yet Thomson Reuters finds only 17% of C-suite peers see legal as a significant contributor versus 86% of GCs, making evidence production a core legal-ops job. Gartner predicts 60% of departments will run AI intake answering half of requests without humans by 2029; Deloitte urges moving risk evaluation "closer to where business occurs." On outside counsel, expectations outrun reality: 64% expect less reliance and 92% expect AI rate cuts, but 59% see no savings, AFAs remain near 20% of matters, fewer than one in five departments have pressed firms, and only 2% of public outside counsel guidelines mention AI. Deloitte respondents expect hourly work to fall from 72% to 44%. New models are real: AI-native firms (Norm Law, Crosby, General Legal, Eudia Counsel) with fixed fees and no junior pyramid, and MSO/private-equity structures (McDermott). Scaling lags: only 7% have moved AI beyond pilots (Axiom); 84% have not redesigned roles (Deloitte). Gartner, ACC newsroom, and several trade sites were blocked; some quotes need verbatim re-check.

1. **Outside counsel has stopped being the default pressure valve: only 37% of legal departments expect outside counsel spend to increase, down from 58% a year earlier, while demand keeps rising.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-03-02]
   CLOC 2026 State of the Industry Report (data from the 2025 Harbor Law Department Survey with CLOC; 135 departments, 15+ industries, median revenue US$13B). Workload drivers: regulatory compliance 63%, cybersecurity 58%. Inside spend increase expected by 47% (down from 65%); lawyer headcount increase by 32%. Legal ops focus areas: technology strategy 80%, financial management 72%, outside counsel/vendor management 62%. Full report is gated; figures from CLOC press release and corroborated by Lexpert.
   Quote: "Demand is accelerating in areas like regulatory compliance and cybersecurity, yet budget and staffing growth are not keeping pace."
   https://cloc.org/newsdesk/cloc-releases-2026-state-of-the-industry-report-rising-legal-demand-outpaces-budget-and-staffing-growth-forcing-operational-shift/
2. **AI governance structures are near-universal in large departments (85% have a dedicated AI resource or committee), and outside counsel optimization is accelerating: 76% use AFAs, 61% have or are implementing convergence panels, 65% intentionally kept work in-house.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2025-12-08]
   Harbor 2025 Law Department Survey (22nd annual, with CLOC), press release 2025-12-08. AFAs up from 70%; convergence up from 50%. 41% of departments expanded headcount, 45% contracted. Oyango Snell quote attributed in release (extracted via summarizer; verify verbatim).
   Quote: "Legal operations has become the engine that keeps the function running efficiently"
   https://www.prweb.com/releases/harbor-2025-law-department-survey-reveals-surge-in-ai-integration-falling-outside-counsel-spend-302635093.html
3. **The CLO has structurally become a business executive: a record 84% report directly to the CEO, 47% say AI/technology proficiency is what their CEO most wants them to develop, and AI adoption is aimed at role evolution, not headcount cuts.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-01]
   2026 ACC Chief Legal Officers Survey Key Findings PDF (1,049 CLOs, 43 countries, 20 industries), text read directly. Also: 74% provide proactive strategic counsel; 79% almost always attend board meetings; 36% in active GenAI deployment; 63% expect stable headcount; operational efficiency (53%) top strategic initiative; budget/resource constraints top barrier (35%). Counter-signal: for regulatory surges, use of outside counsel rose to 48% and consultants to 27% (ACC calls external resources the regulatory 'pressure valve'). A secondary Substack cites 62% regularly sought for strategic input (up from 43%) - not verified against the gated full report.
   Quote: "The transition from "head lawyer" to "business executive" has reached a structural peak, with a record 84% of CLOs now reporting directly to the CEO."
   https://www.acc.com/sites/default/files/2026-01/2026-ACC-Chief-Legal-Officers-Survey-Key-Findings.pdf
4. **The GC's strategic role is largely self-assessed: 86% of GCs see legal as a significant contributor to business objectives, but only 17% of other C-suite executives agree and 42% say legal contributes little or not at all.** [not fact-checked; confidence high; primary; 2026-03-24]
   Thomson Reuters Institute 2026 State of the Corporate Law Department Report. Also: ~48% of GCs cite staffing/resources as top barrier; 36% expect to raise outside counsel spend, 20% to decrease; technology as strategic priority doubled 14% to 28%; ~49% have enterprise GenAI tools; few departments measure AI success or tie it to business outcomes. Implication: legal ops' job is producing outcome evidence.
   https://www.thomsonreuters.com/en/institute/reports/state-of-the-corporate-law-department-report-2026
5. **Gartner predicts that by 2029, 60% of legal departments will use AI-driven intake that answers half of requests without human intervention, and about 50% of contract reviews will go to self-service systems escalating only one in ten; legal tech budgets double by 2028.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; trade-press; 2026-05-27]
   Gartner press release dated 2026-05-26 (gartner.com returned 403; content and quotes taken from two matching secondary reprints: SMBtech 2026-05-27 and Lawyers Weekly AU 2026-06-09). Analyst: Weston Wicks, Senior Director Analyst, Gartner Legal & Compliance. Quote below is trimmed from a longer sentence and uses the AU reprint's spelling ('per cent'). Gartner also has a paywalled note titled 'Predicts 2026: AI and Agentic AI Will Enable Legal Self-Service'. This is the best available number for the 'legal front door' theme; no neutral current-adoption figure was found.
   Quote: "60 per cent of legal departments will use AI-driven intake systems that capture all requests and answer one-half of those without human intervention"
   https://smbtech.au/news/gartner-predicts-legal-tech-budgets-to-double-by-2028-as-ai-use-expands-across-legal-departments/
6. **Deloitte predicts legal risk evaluation will move out to the business with legal supplying guardrails, that CLOs will view delivery through an 'ecosystem lens', and that non-traditional roles (project managers, data scientists, technology specialists) will become common in legal departments.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026]
   Deloitte US CLO Program, '2026 Predictions for Chief Legal Officers' (PDF text read directly; copyright 2026, exact publication date not shown). Also predicts CLOs will pursue centralized data repositories, CLO-CIO collaboration will be crucial, and 'skill expiration' will accelerate. Another exact line: 'Legal departments should consider establishing frameworks, processes, guardrails, and escalation procedures for risk evaluations to happen closer to where business occurs'.
   Quote: "Roles not traditionally found in corporate legal departments are expected to become more common. Examples include project managers, data scientists, and technology specialists."
   https://www.deloitte.com/content/dam/assets-zone3/us/en/docs/programs/2026/us-2026-predictions-for-chief-legal-officers.pdf
7. **Legal leaders expect hourly-rate work to fall from 72% to 44% of external work within 2-3 years and 85% believe AI will change law firm pricing, yet 84% have not redesigned internal roles around AI.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-07-09]
   Deloitte UK, 'The AI Imperative: Reshaping of the Legal Industry'; 121 senior legal leaders (GCs, heads of legal, legal ops directors) surveyed April-May 2026. 61% in AI deployment phases, 10% fully embedded, 2% no adoption (vs 76% in 2024); 61% piloting agentic AI; 79% raised AI investment, average +67%; expect 28% of legal work saved/automated in 2-3 years; 78% cite cost reduction as leading benefit expected from external providers. Tom Brunt quote extracted via summarizer; verify verbatim.
   Quote: "AI is rapidly shifting from a productivity tool to a structural force in the legal sector."
   https://www.deloitte.com/uk/en/about/press-room/ai-set-to-reshape-legal-work-law-firm-pricing-and-legal-careers.html
8. **In-house expectations of AI-driven savings from law firms are far ahead of realized savings: 64% expect reduced reliance on outside counsel and 61% plan to push for pricing/service changes, but 59% have seen no noticeable savings and only 24% are satisfied with firms' GenAI adoption.** [not fact-checked; confidence high; primary; 2025-10-14]
   ACC/Everlaw GenAI survey, 657 in-house professionals in 30 countries, fieldwork June 18-July 18 2025, released 2025-10-14. GenAI active use 52% vs 23% in 2024; 50% expect lower outside counsel costs; planned insourcing of drafting 78%, contract management 71%, research 62%; policy bans fell from 29% to 9%. Bloomberg Law coverage adds Gartner's Weston Wicks: 'Law firms don't know how to charge clients for using AI tools' (first clause of a longer sentence).
   https://www.everlaw.com/press/release/acc-report-2025/
9. **Legal departments are ahead of their law firms on GenAI (legal research: 72.3% in-house vs 23.4% at firms) and 93.8% say AI will let them do more in-house, but fewer than one in five have pressed firms to demonstrate AI-driven savings and fewer than 40% of legal ops leaders control selection of AI legal assistants.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; aggregator; 2025-12-19]
   Blickstein Group / FTI Consulting 18th annual Law Department Operations Survey (68 mostly North American companies). SECONDARY: reported by Legal.io citing Legaltech News; the full Blickstein report was not retrievable. 87.5% expect reduced law firm spend; about two-thirds report ALSP spend declined; only about one-third can show measurable AI cost savings. Brad Blickstein's statement that departments lack a clear strategy for what they want from firms may be a paraphrase - verify before quoting.
   https://www.legal.io/blog/5770721/In-house-Legal-Teams-Pull-Ahead-Of-Law-Firms-On-Generative-AI-Adoption
   FACT-CHECK: Use 'nearly 94%' (as published by Legal.io) rather than 93.8%. Blickstein statement is a paraphrase - do not put it in quotation marks. Small sample (68). Fetched the Legal.io article (December 19, 2025); it is secondary and cites Legaltech News. Seen: 72.3% vs 23.4%; 87.5% expect to spend less on law firms (up about 20 points year over year); 'Fewer than one in five respondents reported pressing outside counsel'; 'Fewer than 40% said they have primary responsibility for selecting AI-powered legal assistants'; nearly two-thirds say ALSP spend declined, half expect further decreases; one-third could show measurable savings (two-thirds non-financial benefits); 68 mostly North American companies; 18th annual. Legal.io says 'Nearly 94%', not 93.8% - the 93.8% figure was not seen anywhere. Brad Blickstein's statement is a paraphrase in Legal.io (no quotation marks): he 'told Legaltech News that many law departments lack a clear strategy for what they want firms to deliver using AI'. Primary Blickstein report not obtained.
10. **Despite a decade of talk, AFAs cover only about 20% of legal matters and formal law firm panels remain rare (16%); legal ops is broadening from cost control to systems, process and technology, but in 45% of cases the 'legal ops' lead is a GC doing it on the side.** [not fact-checked; confidence high; primary; 2025-09]
   Thomson Reuters Institute 2025 Legal Department Operations Index (PDF text read directly; July 2025 survey, 128 US respondents, with Buying Legal Council). 82% have at least one dedicated legal ops role; 56% under-resourced; 46% expect more work in-house; 73% plan to automate with advanced tech; 59% aim to improve legal-business collaboration; 70% expect GenAI to change interactions with internal stakeholders. Departments led by legal-ops professionals use more tech, ALSPs and AFAs than those led by GCs tasked with ops. Report says AFA expansion 'still remains largely a discussion rather than a plan of action'.
   Quote: "AFAs are used in about 20% of legal matters consistently."
   https://www.thomsonreuters.com/en-us/posts/wp-content/uploads/sites/20/2025/09/Legal-Department-Operations-Index-2025.pdf
11. **AI adoption is widespread but scaling is rare: only 7% of in-house teams have moved past pilots to using, optimizing and measuring AI; 83% cannot show whether last year's AI spend paid off; 92% expect or negotiate AI-related rate cuts from outside counsel; 52% prefer ALSPs over law firms (24%) for AI-enabled work.** [not fact-checked; confidence medium; vendor; 2026-07-09]
   Axiom 2026 Legal AI Survey, 528 in-house legal leaders (CLOs, GCs, DGCs, legal ops) in six countries, fielded by InsightDynamo; 77% from $1B+ companies. 66% run general-purpose AI in default configuration. Axiom is an ALSP and an interested party. Companion Axiom 2026 GC Survey (2026-02-10): 80% plan to move significant law firm work in-house or to ALSPs within 24 months. Note new title at Axiom: 'Chief Legal AI and Talent Officer' (Sara Morgan). Quote extracted via summarizer; verify.
   Quote: "The technology is not the hard part. The layer around it is where value either shows up or it doesn't."
   https://www.axiomlaw.com/resources/press-releases/legal-ai-is-everywhere-but-only-7-of-legal-teams-have-made-it-work
12. **Client AI expectations have barely reached the contract layer: only 2% (20 of 1,054) of publicly findable outside counsel guidelines mention AI.** [not fact-checked; confidence medium; other; 2026-09-16]
   Fulkerson Advisors study of OCGs dated Dec 2022 or later found via search engines, company/insurer/public-body sites, procurement portals and court dockets. Of the 20: 12 require disclosure of AI use, 9 keep client data out of public tools, 8 require lawyer review, 9 change billing, 3 require prior approval, 1 bans AI. Named: Microsoft (2024), Zscaler (2025), UBS, The Hartford, FDIC (2026). Small consultancy; open-web sample skews toward public bodies - but it is the only hard count found. Contrast with KPMG 2026 GC Outlook: 82% expect firms to explain AI usage clearly.
   https://www.fulkersonadvisors.com/research/ai-in-outside-counsel-guidelines
13. **CLOC's 2026 Global Institute positioned legal ops as 'the strategic architecture behind the modern legal department'; the AI conversation shifted from possibility to governance, and CLOC launched the Compass maturity tool on the Core 12.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; trade-press; 2026-05-15]
   CLOC Global Institute, Chicago, May 11-14 2026, ~2,400 attendees from 26+ countries, 90+ sessions, theme 'Stronger by Design'. Program themes: AI training and governance, resource optimization/spend management, career pathways, skills, organizational design. CLOC Compass built with Neota Logic. Legal IT Insider's Toby Weston: vendors are racing to become the legal department 'operating system' with embedded AI; open question is organizational readiness. Snell quote matched in two sources. A second Snell quote (Lexpert + CCBJ): 'Legal operations leaders aren't waiting for permission anymore. They're building, proving, and leading.'
   Quote: "The AI conversation has matured. Teams are now sharing what has worked, what broke, and how they are governing it."
   https://legaltechnology.com/cloc-global-institute-2026-a-market-in-transition/
   FACT-CHECK: Attribute 'the strategic architecture behind the modern legal department' to CLOC's press release (its own self-description), and cite https://cloc.org/newsdesk/2026-cloc-global-institute-brings-more-than-2300/ for attendance (nearly 2,400), 26+ countries and 90+ sessions. The cited Legal IT Insider article (Toby Weston, May 15, 2026) contains the exact Snell quote ('The AI conversation has matured. Teams are now sharing what has worked, what broke, and how they are governing it.'), the Compass / Neota Logic / Core 12 launch, 'over 2,400' attendees, and 'The race to become the operating system for legal departments is underway'. It does NOT contain 'strategic architecture', '26 countries', '90 sessions' or the dates. Those are in CLOC's own press release of May 15, 2026: held May 11-14 at McCormick Place West, Chicago; 'nearly 2,400' attendees (headline says 'More Than 2,300'); more than 26 countries; more than 90 sessions; theme 'Stronger by Design, underscoring the evolving role of legal operations as the strategic architecture behind the modern legal department'. That phrase is CLOC press-release narrative, not a Snell quote and not Legal IT Insider's words. Second Snell quote confirmed in the CLOC release, full form: 'Legal operations leaders aren't waiting for permission anymore. They're building, proving, and leading, and CGI 2026 showed that in full.' Lexpert and CCBJ not checked.
14. **New in-house roles such as 'legal engineer' are emerging inside legal ops teams, but titles, pay and headcount approval lag the work.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; trade-press; 2026-06-08]
   Law360 Pulse (Anna Scott Farrell). Examples: John Deere legal ops; an unnamed large pharma seeking a legal engineer for its legal ops team. Mary O'Carroll (LegalEng; former Google legal ops head and CLOC president): new, poorly understood roles make headcount approval harder. Practitioner corroboration: Gio DiLuca (Senior Director Legal Ops, Lowe's) in ACC Corporate Counsel Now, 2026-03-03, lists 'from innovator to AI governance steward' and 'from vendor management to ecosystem orchestration' among ten shifts. Quotes extracted via summarizer; verify.
   Quote: "From what I see, the legal engineer is already in-house. We just haven't seen the title, salary shift yet."
   https://www.law360.com/pulse/articles/2487125/ai-boom-gives-rise-to-in-house-legal-engineers
   FACT-CHECK: Quote speaker: Elly Meenan, founder, The Legal Ops Job Board. Use the O'Carroll quote above for the headcount-approval point. DiLuca corroboration unverified. Fetched the Law360 Pulse article: June 8, 2026, Anna Scott Farrell, headline exact. The exact_quote is verbatim but was said by Elly Meenan, founder of The Legal Ops Job Board - the finding names no speaker and could be misread as Mary O'Carroll. O'Carroll (heads consulting firm LegalEng; led legal operations at Google for over a decade) verbatim: 'With these roles that are new and not well understood, it's even more challenging to get that headcount and get it approved.' She also called the hire 'a no-brainer investment'. John Deere example is Elliot Cobb (joined in 2020 to help launch the legal ops team; adopted the 'legal engineer' title when she left in March). The large-pharma example is sourced to Matt Wheatley of Priori Legal. Article also records scepticism about the title (Adina Newman). Her CLOC presidency was not stated in what I saw. The Gio DiLuca / ACC piece (2026-03-03) was NOT verified.
15. **AI-native law firms and hybrid AI/ALSP/law-firm providers are an established new delivery category: fixed fees, AI-first review, senior-only lawyers and no junior pyramid, enabled by Arizona ABS and UK SRA approvals; separately, Big Law is exploring MSO structures to take private equity.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; other; 2025-12-11]
   IBA article (2025-12-11): Norm Law ($140M+ funding, Blackstone-backed, 35+ lawyers as 'legal engineers'), Crosby (58-minute median contract turnaround; later $60M Series B ~2026-04), Covenant ($900/document LPA review, ~90% below traditional), Garfield.Law (SRA-approved May 2025), Eudia Counsel (Arizona ABS). Artificial Lawyer 2026-03-31: General Legal charges $500 per contract review, $250 per NDA, 2.2-hour turnaround, 40-50% margins, hires only experienced lawyers. Artificial Lawyer 2026-01-14: Eudia (AI platform + ALSP + law firm; $105M Series A led by General Catalyst; >$20M ARR by Dec 2025). ABA Journal 2026-02-10: McDermott Will & Schulte in preliminary talks on an investor-owned MSO. Baseline: TR ALSP 2025 report sized ALSPs at $28.5B, law-firm captives only $1.8B (2025-01).
   https://www.ibanet.org/AI-native-law-firm-regulatory-innovation-and-fundamental-restructuring-of-legal-service-delivery
   FACT-CHECK: $140M+ is NormAI's total funding (Norm Law got $50M from Blackstone). ALSP $28.5B is a 2023 market size published Jan 2025. McDermott MSO, Crosby Series B and Eudia Series A details are unverified - do not publish without a source. IBA article fetched (11 December 2025; authors Shreya Vajpei and Saranya Mishra). Seen: 'NormAI's total funding exceeds $140m' (Blackstone, Bain Capital, Vanguard, Citi, Marc Benioff) - that is the parent NormAI, not Norm Law; Norm Law received a $50m Blackstone investment in Nov 2025; 'over 35 lawyers'; Crosby 58-minute median turnaround ($5.8m seed from Sequoia/Bain Capital Ventures); Covenant $900 per document, about 90% below traditional, six lawyers; Garfield.Law SRA-approved 6 May 2025; Eudia Counsel under Arizona ABS. The 'zero junior lawyers' example in the IBA piece is Pierson Ferdinand. IBA does not mention MSOs, private equity or McDermott. Artificial Lawyer 2026-03-31 ('How Do AI-Native Law Firms Work?') confirms General Legal: $500 per contract, $250 NDA, 2.2 hours for an MSA vs 8-10, '40 or 50 %' margins, experienced hires only (firm was under three months old, ~110 clients). Artificial Lawyer 2026-01-14 confirms Eudia = AI company + ALSP + law firm and crossing $20M ARR in December 2025; the $105M Series A / General Catalyst is NOT in that article and was not verified this session. TR ALSP 2025 report (January 28, 2025; PDF read): $28.5B as of 2023, 18% CAGR; Figure 2 shows law firm captives/affiliates $1.8B, Big Four $1.6B, independents $25.1B - confirmed, but note the figure is for 2023. NOT verified: ABA Journal 2026-02-10 McDermott Will & Schulte MSO talks; Crosby $60M Series B. 'Established category' is the researcher's characterisation; most of these firms are under two years old.

Viewpoints:
- CLOC / Harbor (Oyango Snell, Lauren Chung, Kevin Clem): a structural productivity gap exists; outside counsel is no longer the pressure valve; legal ops is 'the strategic architecture' and 'the engine' of the department; the AI conversation has matured into governance.
- ACC (2026 CLO Survey): the CLO is now a business executive mandated to lead AI transformation while resource-starved; AI targets role evolution rather than headcount reduction; outside counsel remains the 'pressure valve' for regulatory surges - in tension with CLOC's framing.
- Thomson Reuters Institute: the strategic-GC narrative is self-assessed (86% of GCs vs 17% of C-suite); legal must prove outcomes, not tasks; AFAs and formal panels have barely moved in a decade despite the AI pricing talk.
- Gartner (Weston Wicks): the end state is business self-service and AI intake (60% of departments by 2029); multi-agent legal applications reduce reliance on external counsel; neither firms nor clients yet know how to price AI-assisted work.
- Deloitte (US CLO Program; Tom Brunt, Deloitte Legal UK): AI is a structural force; hourly billing share expected to fall from 72% to 44%; 84% have not redesigned roles; move risk evaluation closer to where business occurs; view delivery through an ecosystem lens rather than inside vs outside.
- Blickstein Group (Brad Blickstein): departments are ahead of their firms on AI but lack a strategy for what to demand from firms; fewer than one in five have pressed for AI savings; many legal ops leaders do not control AI tool selection.
- Axiom (ALSP, interested party; Daniel Hayter, Chris Frickland): law firm panels are obsolete and should be replaced by per-matter talent/technology/price decisions; 80% will move work away from firms; only 7% have made AI work because the operating layer around the tools is the hard part.
- Legal AI vendors (Daniel Lewis of LegalOn; Omar Haroun of Eudia): billable-hour incentives mean in-house teams, not law firms, will capture AI productivity; in-house needs are 'at odds with' law firm needs.
- AI-native law firms (General Legal's JP Mohler; Crosby; Norm Law; Covenant): AI is the first reviewer, lawyers supervise like partners, pricing is fixed, only experienced lawyers are hired - no junior pyramid.
- Skeptics: Above the Law and ABA Journal experts say AI is a convenient label for ordinary cost-cutting (Baker McKenzie) and law firm revenues are still booming; Forrester (via National Law Review) expects 25% of planned AI spend deferred to 2027; Mary O'Carroll notes new legal-engineering roles are hard to get headcount for; Juro data shows 84% of in-house lawyers see no fee reductions from firm AI use.
- Practitioner (Gio DiLuca, Senior Director Legal Operations, Lowe's): the head of legal ops has moved from efficiency manager to strategic partner, platform architect, ecosystem orchestrator, executive storyteller and AI governance steward.

Gaps:
- No neutral, survey-based current adoption figure for legal front door / intake portals / NDA self-service in 2025-2026; only Gartner's 2029 prediction (via secondary reprints), Juro's vendor survey (13% already running routine contracts without daily legal involvement) and vendor marketing.
- CLOC 2026 State of the Industry full report is gated; no CLOC data retrieved on legal ops team size, reporting lines, or who specifically owns AI governance. CLOC Global Institute 2026 session-level content and CLOC Compass detail beyond launch facts not retrieved.
- All gartner.com pages returned 403; Gartner statistics and Weston Wicks quotes come from secondary reprints (SMBtech, Lawyers Weekly AU, Bloomberg Law). The Gartner note 'Predicts 2026: AI and Agentic AI Will Enable Legal Self-Service' and the Dec 2025 release 'Only 20% of Legal Matters Sent to Outside Counsel Stay Within Budget Range' are known by title only.
- ACC full 2026 CLO report is gated and the ACC newsroom page returned 403; the '62% regularly sought for strategic input (up from 43%)' figure comes from a Substack and is unverified. The ACC 2025 Law Department Management Benchmarking figure (median firms used fell from 14 to 10) is from a search snippet; no 2026 edition confirmed; nothing new found on the ACC Legal Operations Maturity Model.
- Blickstein Group 2025 LDO Survey full report not retrieved; all figures are secondary (Legal.io citing Legaltech News), and the Brad Blickstein quote may be a paraphrase.
- EY Law General Counsel Study: only the April 2025 edition was found (figures from search snippets); no 2026 edition located.
- No hard data on law firm associate class reductions or partner-role restructuring; only commentary (Axios, Forbes, Lawyer Monthly) that could not be fetched. Baker McKenzie layoffs details are from search snippets, not fetched articles.
- No survey evidence that legal ops specifically owns knowledge curation; only indirect signals (Deloitte centralized data repositories, Axiom's 66% running default-configured AI, vendor 'institutional knowledge capture' features).
- No 2026 market sizing for managed legal services or law-firm subsidiaries; the latest ALSP sizing is Thomson Reuters' January 2025 report ($28.5B; captives $1.8B).
- Several quotes were extracted through a summarizing fetch model rather than read verbatim (flagged 'EXTRACTED' in notes); they need a verbatim check against the live page before publication. Quotes read directly from PDFs (ACC Key Findings, Deloitte US predictions, TR LDO Index) are exact.
- The session's WebSearch budget was exhausted after about 28 searches, so some planned searches (Gartner Dec 2025 release, Law.com Corporate Counsel coverage, LawNext) were replaced with direct fetches; Law.com article was paywalled.

Fact-checker overall: I fetched all 15 cited URLs and read the headline claim on every one. No finding was refuted. 8 are confirmed and 7 are corrected, and none is unverified at the headline level. No web search was possible because the session's search budget (200 of 200) was already spent, so any sub-claim that needed a search to locate stays unverified. For the three PDFs (ACC, Deloitte US, TR Operations Index) I extracted the text with pdftotext and read it directly. I also found and read the TR ALSP 2025 report PDF and CLOC's own Global Institute recap release. No file in any git repository was changed.

**Corrections that matter most**
- **Finding 14 (Law360):** the "legal engineer is already in-house" quote is by Elly Meenan of The Legal Ops Job Board, not Mary O'Carroll.
- **Finding 11 (Axiom):** the quote is by Chris Frickland, VP AI Solutions, not Sara Morgan.
- **Finding 11 (Axiom):** the companion GC report is dated 2026-02-17, not 2026-02-10.
- **Finding 13 (CLOC):** the "strategic architecture" phrase is CLOC's own press-release language from 2026-05-15. It is not in Legal IT Insider and it is not a Snell quote.
- **Finding 4 (Thomson Reuters):** the page only says technology as a priority "doubled". The specific 14% to 28% figures were not seen.
- **Finding 9 (Blickstein via Legal.io):**
  - Legal.io says "nearly 94%", not 93.8%.
  - The Blickstein statement is a paraphrase and should not go in quotation marks.
- **Finding 15 (AI-native firms):** the $140M+ is NormAI's total funding, not Norm Law's.
- **Finding 15 (AI-native firms):** the $28.5B ALSP market size is a 2023 figure.
- **Finding 8 (ACC/Everlaw):** the release says "no GenAI savings ... yet". The word "noticeable" is not there.
- **Finding 6 (Deloitte US):** the risk-evaluation line is a recommendation ("should consider"), not a prediction.
- **Finding 10 (TR Operations Index):** the 20% AFA figure is TR's decade-long observation, not a 2025 survey result.

**Still unverified because no search was possible**
- The Bloomberg Law quote from Weston Wicks.
- The ACC/Everlaw fieldwork dates.
- The KPMG 2026 GC Outlook 82% figure.
- The Gio DiLuca piece in ACC Corporate Counsel Now.
- The ABA Journal report on McDermott MSO talks.
- Crosby's $60M Series B and Eudia's $105M Series A.
- The Substack figure on CLOs sought for strategic input (62% versus 43%).
- The Lexpert and CCBJ corroborations.
- The Gartner primary press release and its 2026-05-26 date (gartner.com returns 403). The Gartner content itself was confirmed on two trade reprints.

One WebFetch summary of the TR ALSP page offered a report PDF link on an unrelated third-party CDN. I did not use it and read Thomson Reuters' own hosted PDF instead.

Verification notes are in C:/Users/RALPHS~1/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--code-files-spaarke-website/aa6b682a-4844-485c-99e0-cb2228b928c3/scratchpad/research/changing-role-of-legal-ops.verified.md. The extracted PDF texts are in the same folder: acc-clo-2026.txt, deloitte-clo-2026.txt, tr-ldo-2025.txt and tr-alsp-2025.txt.


## research:ai-writing-tells

The best current catalog is Wikipedia's "Signs of AI writing" (WikiProject AI Cleanup, revised 2026-09-21), backed by practitioner lists (tropes.fyi, Feb 2026; Rough at Sea's LLM-ism Dictionary, Aug 2026) and peer-reviewed work. Kobak et al. (Science Advances, July 2025) found at least 13.5% of 2024 PubMed abstracts were LLM-processed, with "delves" at 28 times expected frequency. Reinhart et al. (PNAS 2025) measured present-participial clauses at 2 to 5 times human rates, the evidence behind the "highlighting the importance of" tail. Russell et al. (ACL 2025) showed frequent LLM users detect AI text almost perfectly, citing vocabulary (53%) and sentence structure (36%) most. Vocabulary tells are fading ("delve" dropped sharply in 2025) while negative parallelism persists (The Atlantic, July 2026, secondary access only). The em dash is weak evidence of authorship (Twain exceeds most models) but a strong public trigger; Freeburg (arXiv, March 2026) measured 0 to 10.6 per 1,000 words across models against a human mean of 3.2, and OpenAI treated it as a defect (Nov 2025). Style authorities agree every em dash function has a more formal substitute. The notes file contains a five-group taxonomy with invented business-register rewrites, an em dash decision guide with six examples plus rules for en dashes, spaced hyphens and double hyphens, and a ranked view of which tells matter to expert readers: content emptiness first, manufactured-drama constructions second, template rhythm third, dashes fourth, vocabulary last. Wikipedia's warning frames everything: fix the underlying vagueness, not only the surface sign. Search budget ran out after 15 searches; several paywalled primaries were unreachable.

1. **Wikipedia's 'Signs of AI writing' is the most complete, actively maintained catalog, and it warns that the signs are symptoms, not the problem to fix.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-21]
   Living document by WikiProject AI Cleanup (project formed 2023); most recent revision observed 2026-09-21. Groups tells under Content (inflated significance, superficial '-ing' analyses, promotional tone, vague attribution, 'Despite its... challenges' conclusions), Language (AI vocabulary, copula avoidance such as 'serves as', negative parallelism in three subtypes, rule of three) and Style (title case, mechanical bold, inline bold-header lists, em dashes). It also cautions that not all text with these features is AI-written.
   Quote: "Please do not merely treat these signs as the problems to be fixed; that could just make detection harder."
   https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing
2. **AI vocabulary tells shift by model generation; 'delve' has largely receded, so word lists age quickly and density matters more than any single word.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-21]
   Wikipedia's era table: 2023 to mid-2024 (GPT-4) includes delve, tapestry, testament, landscape, pivotal, crucial, underscore, intricate, interplay, vibrant; mid-2024 to mid-2025 (GPT-4o) includes align with, fostering, enhance, highlighting, showcasing; mid-2025 on (GPT-5) shrinks to emphasizing, enhance, highlighting, showcasing. The page says delve dropped off sharply in 2025.
   Quote: "an edit (post-2022) introducing lots of them, lots of times, is one of the strongest tells for AI use"
   https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing
3. **Peer-reviewed word-frequency analysis found at least 13.5% of 2024 PubMed abstracts were processed with LLMs, detected through excess 'style' words.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2025-07-02]
   Kobak, Gonzalez-Marquez, Horvat and Lause analyzed over 15 million PubMed abstracts (2010 to 2024). Per arXiv v3: 454 excess words in 2024; unlike Covid-era excess words (content nouns), 2024's were mostly style words, 66% verbs and 14% adjectives. Frequency ratios: delves 28.0, underscores 13.8, showcasing 10.7; largest gaps among common words: potential, findings, crucial. Lower bound reaches 30 to 40% in some subcorpora. Science Advances page returned 403, so figures are from arXiv versions.
   Quote: "at least 13.5% of 2024 abstracts were processed with LLMs"
   https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.07016
4. **The present-participle tail and noun-heavy density are measurable: instruction-tuned LLMs use present participial clauses at 2 to 5 times the human rate.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2025-08-21]
   Reinhart et al. applied Biber's feature set to GPT-4o and Llama 3 variants. GPT-4o: present participial clauses 5.3x human rate, nominalizations 2.1x, 'that' subject clauses 2.6x, phrasal coordination 1.9x, agentless passives about half. Overused words in GPT-4o by rate multiple: camaraderie 162, tapestry 155, intricate 119. Base Llama models were close to human rates; instruction tuning produced the divergence.
   Quote: "Instruction tuning appears to make the model output less human, not more"
   https://arxiv.org/abs/2410.16107
5. **Frequent LLM users are near-perfect detectors of AI text, and the clues they cite most are vocabulary and sentence structure, followed by punctuation, originality, quotations, over-explanation, formatting and formula conclusions.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; 2025-01-26]
   Russell, Karpinska and Iyyer (ACL 2025): 300 articles; majority vote of five expert annotators misclassified 1 of 300; non-experts were near chance (56.7% TPR). Clue shares: vocabulary 53.1%, sentence structure 35.9% (including lists of three), grammar and punctuation 24.8%, originality 23.7%, quotations 22.3%, clarity 19.5%, formatting 15.0% (balanced paragraphs, consistent headers), conclusions 13.1% (repetitive, optimistic). Non-experts wrongly flagged any formal or correct prose. Implies that Spaarke's complaining readers, likely heavy LLM users, are accurate judges.
   Quote: "quotes feel fake, every expert speaks the same way and it's too homogenous with the text"
   https://arxiv.org/html/2501.15654v1
   FACT-CHECK: Quote is not exact. The paper reads: "The quotes also feel fake, every expert speaks the same way and it's too homogenous with the text." Also, the 'Grammar & Punctuation' category (24.8%) is defined as AI text being 'usually grammatically perfect (also avoiding dashes and ellipses)', so in this study dashes were read as a human marker, which cuts against using it to support the em dash tell. All numbers seen in arXiv HTML: 300 articles; majority vote of five experts 'misclassifies only 1 of 300'; nonexperts avg TPR 56.7% (FPR 52.5% in text, 51.7 in table); experts avg TPR 92.7%, FPR 4.0%; clue shares 53.1 / 35.9 / 24.8 / 23.7 / 22.3 / 19.5 / 15.0 / 13.1 all match. Formatting is defined as 'fully capitalized headings, bolded lists, paragraphs of similar length'. Models tested were GPT-4o, Claude 3.5 Sonnet and o1-Pro. ACL 2025 long paper pp. 5342-5373, DOI 10.18653/v1/2025.acl-long.267. The closing inference about Spaarke's readers is the researcher's, not the paper's.
6. **Em dash frequency is model-specific rather than universal: 0 to 10.6 per 1,000 words across twelve models, against a human essay mean of 3.23 with a 50-fold human range.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; primary; 2026-03-27]
   Freeburg (arXiv preprint, not evidently peer reviewed): GPT-4.1 10.62, Claude Opus 4.6 9.09, DeepSeek V3 6.95, Gemini 2.5 Pro 3.53, Llama 0.00. Human baseline from eight published essays (57,232 words): mean 3.23, range 0.33 to 17.12. Under suppression instructions GPT-4.1 stayed at 9.10 while Claude Opus fell to 0.19. Hypothesis: the em dash is markdown structure leaking into prose, shaped by fine-tuning.
   Quote: "the em dash is markdown leaking into prose"
   https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27006
7. **Wikipedia's em dash tell is about placement as well as frequency: models use em dashes where people would use commas, parentheses or colons, which maps directly onto a replacement guide.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-21]
   The em dash section also notes that AI em dashes are often spaced, and cites an unidentified July 2026 study finding that among contemporary models only Claude exceeded professional writers' em dash rate while ChatGPT fell below it. The observation that humans more often use '(misused) hyphens and en dashes' explains why swapping glyphs can look superficially human but is still poor practice in formal publishing.
   Quote: "uses them in places where humans are more likely to use commas, parentheses, colons, or (misused) hyphens (-) and en dashes (–)"
   https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Signs_of_AI_writing
   FACT-CHECK: The 'unidentified July 2026 study' is identified in the page's reference: The Economist, 'How to spot AI writing', 30 July 2026 (https://www.economist.com/culture/2026/07/30/how-to-spot-ai-writing). Wikipedia says AI em dashes are 'usually surrounded by spaces' (not 'often'). A September 2026 banner now heads the section saying the sign 'seems to be less common in current LLM output' and may be moved to historical indicators. Quote exact in the wikitext; the full sentence compares LLM output with 'nonprofessional human-written text of the same genre'. Wikipedia sentence on the study: 'of contemporary models only Claude used em dashes more than professional writers, and ChatGPT used them less.' I could not open The Economist (403, no Wayback copy), so its underlying numbers are unverified. The section also says the sign 'is most useful when taken in combination with other indicators, not by itself'.
8. **OpenAI publicly treated ChatGPT's em dash habit as a defect to fix, confirming the mark's status as a recognized tell.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; trade-press; 2025-11-14]
   On 2025-11-14 Sam Altman announced that ChatGPT now obeys custom instructions not to use em dashes. TechCrunch (Sarah Perez) reported the mark had become a telltale sign of AI text in school papers, emails and social posts. Default behavior without an instruction was unchanged.
   Quote: "If you tell ChatGPT not to use em-dashes in your custom instructions, it finally does what it's supposed to do!"
   https://techcrunch.com/2025/11/14/openai-says-its-fixed-chatgpts-em-dash-problem/
9. **Style authorities agree every em dash function has a more formal substitute: comma pair, parentheses, colon, semicolon or full stop.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; other; unknown]
   The Punctuation Guide says the em dash replaces commas, parentheses or colons, is more emphatic than commas, less formal than parentheses and the colon, and should be limited to two per sentence. Chicago Manual of Style Q&A concedes that between two independent clauses 'a semicolon or a period might be better than a dash' and advises editing dashes out when in doubt. Wikipedia's Dash article documents the spaced en dash as British house style (Penguin, CUP, Routledge), the double hyphen as a typewriter proxy, and the en dash for closed numeric ranges.
   Quote: "Dashes are considered less formal than parentheses; they are also more intrusive."
   https://www.thepunctuationguide.com/em-dash.html
10. **The em dash is weak evidence of authorship even though it is a strong trigger of suspicion; defenders note that canonical human prose matches or exceeds model rates.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; vendor; 2026-07-31]
   SlopDetector (a detection vendor) counted 10.13 em dashes per 1,000 words in Huckleberry Finn and 8.12 in Moby-Dick versus GPT-4.1's 10.62, concluding the gap is 'much smaller and noisier than the myth suggests' and weighting the signal low. Brian Phillips in The Ringer (2025-08-20) argues models copied the mark from skilled human writers. The vendor blog describes Freeburg's paper as peer-reviewed; arXiv shows no such indication.
   Quote: "A dash is a keystroke, not a thought."
   https://slopdetector.org/blog/em-dash-ai-tell-data
   FACT-CHECK: The phrase 'much smaller and noisier than the myth suggests' is not on the page; actual wording: 'It's just a lot smaller and a lot noisier than "AI uses em dashes and humans don't."' Also, as fetched today the page never uses the word 'peer'; it labels Freeburg '(arXiv, 2026)', 'a controlled study' and 'the cleanest public measurement we found'. Drop the statement that the vendor calls it peer-reviewed. Numbers seen: Huckleberry Finn 10.13, Moby-Dick 8.12, pooled 4.76 em dashes per 1,000 over 702,939 words, GPT-4.1 10.62 (taken from Freeburg). 'A dash is a keystroke, not a thought.' exact; 'weighted deliberately low' on page; dated Jul 31, 2026. The Ringer confirmed: Brian Phillips, 'Stop AI-Shaming Our Precious, Kindly Em Dashes—Please', 2025-08-20, arguing models learned the mark from human books. Comparators are nineteenth-century novels, not business prose; source is a detection vendor.
11. **Negative parallelism ('it is not X, it is Y') is reported as the most durable AI tell, about three times more frequent in LLM text than human text, and is spreading into corporate filings.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; aggregator; 2026-07-12]
   Source is Will Oremus, The Atlantic, 2026-07-12, which could not be fetched. Wikipedia's 'Negative parallelism' article relies solely on it for the threefold figure. An aggregator summary reports a Barron's count of the construction in Fortune 500 filings rising from about 50 (2023) to over 200 (2025), a Washington Post dataset finding 'not just X, but Y' in about 6% of ChatGPT messages in one July, and that the pattern shows 'no signs of abating' unlike 'delve'. All figures need verification at source. Wikipedia's Signs page separately documents three subtypes.
   https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Negative_parallelism
   FACT-CHECK: The threefold figure is a Pangram (AI-detection vendor) estimate quoted by The Atlantic, and it covers 'Not just X but Y' sentences: 'three times as often in AI writing as they do in human writing'. The Atlantic reports Barron's found the construction 'in corporate communications more than quadrupled from 2023 to 2025'; Barron's lede names conference calls, shareholder letters and news releases, not 'Fortune 500 filings', and the raw counts (about 50 to 200+) were not seen (paywall). Washington Post wording is '6 percent of chats in July' (July 2025), not messages. 'Most durable' is the researcher's gloss; The Atlantic says 'perhaps the best-known tic'. I retrieved the opening of The Atlantic piece directly (Will Oremus, published 2026-07-12): the Pangram sentence, the Barron's sentence and 'has shown no signs of abating' (contrasted with delve) are verbatim. Pangram primary page: 'AI uses phrases that fit this template three times as often as humans' (https://www.pangram.com/supporting-evidence). Barron's: Shaina Mishkin, 'AI Is Changing How Companies Talk to Shareholders. Here Is the Red Flag for Readers.', 2026-04-14. WaPo (Merrill, Chen, Kumer, 2025-11-13, via Wayback): 328,744 GPT-4o messages from 37,929 conversations, May 2024 to July 2025. The Wikipedia 'Negative parallelism' article is a one-source stub; cite the primaries instead.
12. **The cause of overused vocabulary points to human-feedback training rather than architecture or training data, which explains why the tells read as 'trying to sound good'.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2024-12-16]
   Juzek and Ward (COLING 2025) identified 21 focal words in scientific abstracts and found no evidence that architecture, algorithms or training data cause the overuse; model testing was consistent with RLHF. Their follow-up (BIAS 2025 workshop, 2025-08-03) showed experimentally with Llama that human raters systematically prefer text containing some overused words, and framed this as misalignment between annotation workers and end users. Competing accounts for the em dash: old digitized books (Goedecke, 2025-10-30) and markdown leakage (Freeburg).
   Quote: "We fail to find evidence that lexical overrepresentation is caused by model architecture, algorithm choices, or training data."
   https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.11385
13. **Practitioner catalogs cover the structural tells that lack academic measurement, and they stress that clustering and repetition, not single uses, give prose away.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; other; 2026-02]
   tropes.fyi (launched Feb 2026) lists negative parallelism, em-dash addiction, 'The X? A Y.' self-answered questions, false ranges, short punchy fragments, 'Here's the kicker', 'Let's break this down', title case headings, bold-first bullets, fractal summaries, signposted conclusions and the tie-back. Rough at Sea's LLM-ism Dictionary (2026-08-15) adds the 'colon epiphany', 'suspiciously even paragraph lengths', paragraphs where 'Every thought receives a landing sentence', the 'conclusion echo' and the 'agency ending', and describes LLM prose as 'statistically well-behaved'.
   Quote: "Remember: any of these patterns used once might be fine. The problem is when multiple tropes appear together or when a single trope is used repeatedly."
   https://tropes.fyi/
14. **AI-preferred vocabulary is entering human speech, so vocabulary-based judgments will increasingly misfire on human writers.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; primary; 2026-07-16]
   Yakura et al. (Max Planck Institute for Human Development and collaborators) analyzed 737,083 hours of podcast audio and ran a preregistered experiment with 496 participants; words such as delve, showcase, boast, intricacies and meticulous rose abruptly in natural speech after ChatGPT's release. Supports ranking vocabulary as the least durable tell.
   https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.01754
15. **In professional settings, perceived heavy AI assistance lowers perceived sincerity, which is the business cost of sounding like AI.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; trade-press; 2025-08-21]
   Cardon and Coman surveyed 1,100 full-time US professionals (International Journal of Business Communication, 2025). Over 80% rated supervisors as sincere when messages showed low AI assistance, versus 40 to 52% with high assistance. Journal page returned 403; details are from HR Dive's report. The study covers workplace messages, so it applies to published thought leadership only by analogy.
   https://www.hrdive.com/news/managers-risk-loss-of-trust-by-over-relying-on-ai-written-messages/758098/

Viewpoints:
- Tells are symptoms of generic, inflated, unsourced prose; scrubbing the surface signs without adding specifics only makes the text harder to flag and no better to read (Wikipedia WikiProject AI Cleanup).
- The em dash is a legitimate mark that models learned from skilled human writers; treating it as a tell harms writers, and canonical prose (Twain, Melville) matches model rates (The Ringer, SlopDetector).
- The em dash is measurably overused by specific models and functions as a fingerprint of fine-tuning choices; the vendor itself treated it as a defect (Freeburg 2026; Altman, Nov 2025).
- Causes are disputed: digitized older books (Goedecke), human-feedback preference for certain words (Juzek and Ward), markdown leakage (Freeburg), post-training on reasoning tasks for negative parallelism (Salvaggio); The Atlantic reportedly calls the cause of negative parallelism unresolved.
- The tells are a moving target: vocabulary lists shrink with each model generation and 'delve' has faded, while negative parallelism persists across all major models.
- Humans are converging on model vocabulary (Yakura et al.) and non-expert readers misread any formal, correct prose as AI (Russell et al.), so false accusations against human writers will rise.
- For expert professional readers the costliest tells are substantive (unsupported significance claims, vague attribution, conclusions that restate) and manufactured-drama constructions, with the em dash a cheap-to-fix trigger and vocabulary the least durable signal (researcher synthesis from the cited evidence).

Gaps:
- The Atlantic article by Will Oremus (2026-07-12) could not be fetched; the threefold negative-parallelism figure, the Barron's Fortune 500 filing count and the Washington Post 6% figure come only via Wikipedia and an aggregator (aiweekly.co) and must be verified at source. The original measurer of the threefold figure was not identified.
- Washington Post (2025-04-09), Rolling Stone, Marketplace (2026-08-24), Science Advances, PMC and Sage pages returned 403, captcha or paywall redirects; arXiv versions and trade-press reports were used instead.
- Sam Kriss's New York Times Magazine essay (2025-12-03) was not read directly; only a Longreads notice and a weak secondary discussion were reachable.
- The 'July 2026 study' that Wikipedia cites on em dash rates by model versus professional writers was not identified.
- No quantitative study was found for colon reveals, one-sentence dramatic paragraphs, uniform paragraph length, title-case headings or bold lead-in bullets; these rest on practitioner catalogs only.
- No study specific to legal-industry or general-counsel readers' reactions to AI-sounding thought leadership was found; the ranking of which tells matter most is inference from general evidence.
- Juzek and Ward's list of 21 focal words was not retrieved. Kobak et al. figures come from arXiv v3 and may differ slightly from the Science Advances version.
- The session-wide web search budget was exhausted after 15 searches in this track; later discovery relied on URLs already surfaced, so authoritative sources such as Garner, the Economist style guide or a CMOS statement on em dashes and AI were not located.
- All quotations were retrieved through a summarizing fetch tool and should be checked verbatim against the live pages before publication.

Fact-checker overall: I checked all 15 findings myself. 11 are confirmed, 4 need corrections (5, 7, 10, 11), and none is refuted. The web search budget was already exhausted, so I located primaries by following citations and by Crossref, OpenAlex and Wayback lookups. All figures taken from papers were checked directly against the papers or Crossref and match.

Corrections to make before publishing:
- **Finding 5 (Russell et al.):** the quote is not exact. The paper reads "The quotes also feel fake, every expert speaks the same way and it's too homogenous with the text." The same paper treats dashes as a human marker (AI text "avoiding dashes and ellipses"), so do not cite it in support of the em dash tell.
- **Finding 7 (Wikipedia em dash section):** the "unidentified July 2026 study" is identified in the page's reference list. It is The Economist, "How to spot AI writing", 30 July 2026. The section now carries a September 2026 banner saying the em dash sign "seems to be less common in current LLM output".
- **Finding 10 (SlopDetector):** the phrase "much smaller and noisier than the myth suggests" is not on the page. The actual wording is "a lot smaller and a lot noisier than 'AI uses em dashes and humans don't.'" The page does not call Freeburg's paper peer-reviewed; it labels it "(arXiv, 2026)". Drop that statement.
- **Finding 11 (negative parallelism):**
  - The threefold figure is a vendor estimate from Pangram, quoted by The Atlantic, and it covers "Not just X but Y" sentences only.
  - The Barron's finding, as The Atlantic reports it, is "corporate communications more than quadrupled from 2023 to 2025". I did not see the raw counts (about 50 to 200+) or the "Fortune 500 filings" framing.
  - The Washington Post figure is "6 percent of chats in July" 2025, not messages.
  - "Most durable" is the research agent's wording, not the source's.

Smaller points:
- **Reinhart:** the PNAS publication date is 2025-02-18; 2025-08-21 is only the arXiv v2 date. The quote is mid-sentence in the paper, so the capital "I" is not original.
- **Kobak:** the 66% verbs and 14% adjectives split applies to the 379 excess style words, not to all 454 excess words.
- **Freeburg:** "50-fold" is the research agent's arithmetic (17.12 / 0.33), not the paper's wording, and the human baseline is only eight essays.
- **tropes.fyi:** the quote is on /tropes-md, not the home page. I could not confirm the February 2026 launch; the oldest changelog entry is 2026-03-14.

Still unverified:
- The body of The Economist article (403, no archived copy).
- The Barron's raw counts.
- Yakura's Max Planck affiliation.
- The Cardon and Coman percentages: I saw them only in HR Dive; the journal abstract confirms n = 1,100 and the direction of the sincerity effect.

Two points that cut against an easy replacement guide:
- The tropes.fyi changelog says "--" is now flagged because AI text increasingly uses it as an em dash substitute.
- The Rough at Sea dictionary says "The em dash is not an AI tell."

I wrote the notes to `C:/Users/RALPHS~1/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--code-files-spaarke-website/aa6b682a-4844-485c-99e0-cb2228b928c3/scratchpad/research/ai-writing-tells.verified.md`. Source downloads are cached in `_factcheck_cache_ai-writing-tells/` beside it. No repository files were touched.


## research:knowledge-management-and-ai

Across 2025-2026 sources, the legal industry's consensus framing is that AI is no longer the constraint; knowledge is. Conference programmes (Inside Practice AI x KM New York, April 2026; Inside Legal KM London, September 2026), named KM leaders (Oz Benamram, Tom Humberstone), trade press (Artificial Lawyer, Legal IT Insider, LawSites) and publishers (LexisNexis, Thomson Reuters) all describe KM shifting from maintaining repositories to orchestrating AI workflows, curating trusted content and assuring data quality. Hard numbers are thin and mostly vendor-sourced: iManage's 3,185-respondent benchmark (85% piloting AI, 17% embedded), Gartner's cross-industry 60% abandonment prediction for projects lacking AI-ready data, and Thomson Reuters data showing in-house gen-AI use (47%) now ahead of firms (41%) and 94% of professionals demanding grounding in authoritative content. Technical evidence: Stanford RegLab found RAG legal tools still hallucinate 17-33%; the Vals legal benchmark was built from law-firm-supplied ideal answers, showing work product becoming evaluation material; Anthropic's legal plugin takes the organization's playbook as its configuration. A notable gap supports the article's thesis: CLOC Core 12 and ACC Maturity Model 2.0 still define KM as human reuse of work product and never mention AI, and ACC's "advanced" bar is one full-time KM professional. I found no body that explicitly names legal operations as the knowledge curator. Risks are well documented: US v. Heppner (privilege), embeddings stripping ethical-wall permissions, notetaker transcripts and retention. Quotes came through a summarizing fetch tool and need re-verification; the search budget ran out before ILTACON 2026 coverage.

1. **The dominant 2026 industry framing is that knowledge, not AI, is the bottleneck for legal AI.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-04]
   Inside Practice built its April 29, 2026 'AI x KM: New York' programme around this line, with chief knowledge officers and KM directors from DLA Piper, Paul Hastings, McDermott, Fried Frank, McGuireWoods and Stradley Ronon speaking. Its September 17, 2026 London KM event page adds: 'The gap between AI potential and AI performance is overwhelmingly a knowledge management problem.' Event-organizer copy, so it reflects how the KM community is positioning itself.
   Quote: "Artificial intelligence is no longer the bottleneck for law firms. Knowledge is."
   https://www.insidepractice.com/ai-x-km-2026
2. **Oz Benamram (SKILLS founder, now Pillsbury Chief AI Officer) says AI exposes poor KM and that clean, structured, context-rich KM becomes decisive.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; trade-press; 2026-01-08]
   In Artificial Lawyer's 2026 predictions, Benamram lists the contents of modern KM as precedents, outcomes and expertise signals under a 'Knowledge as a competitive weapon' heading. Same piece: Winston Weinberg (Harvey) 'In 2026, context wins' and Ed Walters (Clio) 'Out: Prompt engineering. In: Context engineering.' Quote wording returned identically on two fetches.
   Quote: "Clean, structured, context-rich KM (precedents, outcomes, expertise signals) becomes decisive"
   https://www.artificiallawyer.com/2026/01/08/artificial-lawyer-predictions-2026/
3. **The 2026 SKILLS Legal AI Survey (130 of the largest law firms) indicates firms have identified the knowledge-management layer as the next AI priority.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; vendor; 2026-03]
   Benamram notes KM-layer tools (DeepJudge, Ask iManage) have among the highest 'Consider' scores, says RAG infrastructure and data governance will decide AI's long-term success, and that 'AI amplifies the cost of fragmentation.' Survey write-up was published on Harvey's blog on 2026-03-06 with the full dashboard on 2026-03-12; treat as vendor-hosted.
   Quote: "Figuring out RAG infrastructure and data governance will be key to AI's long-term success"
   https://www.harvey.ai/blog/2026-skills-legal-ai-survey-oz-benamram-interview
   FACT-CHECK: The cited Q&A page is dated Mar 13, 2026 ('by Harvey Team'). The 2026-03-06 date and the 2026-03-12 dashboard date belong to the companion Harvey post '2026 SKILLS Legal AI Survey: Where Legal AI is Working' by Megan McMillin (https://www.harvey.ai/blog/2026-skills-survey-where-legal-ai-is-working). Cite the Q&A as 2026-03-13. All quotes verified by raw string-match: 'The "Consider" numbers for DeepJudge and Ask iManage are among the highest in the survey, indicating that firms have identified the knowledge management layer as the next priority. Figuring out RAG infrastructure and data governance will be key to AI's long-term success.' and 'firms are realizing that AI amplifies the cost of fragmentation.' Wording is '130 of the world's largest law firms'. Date detail is off for the cited URL.
4. **At ILTACON 2025 the KM roundtable described the KM role as shifting from maintaining knowledge bases to orchestrating AI workflows and ensuring data quality, possibly requiring more KM hiring.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; trade-press; 2025-08-13]
   Artificial Lawyer relayed Draftwise's notes of a 14-speaker KM roundtable (DLA Piper, Paul Hastings, Goodwin; NetDocuments, iManage, Upland). Also reported: 'POC fatigue' and firm knowledge 'scattered across OneDrive, SharePoint, Teams, and personal folders.' Second-hand reporting of a vendor's notes.
   Quote: "KM professionals are shifting from maintaining knowledge bases to orchestrating AI workflows and ensuring data quality."
   https://www.artificiallawyer.com/2025/08/13/iltacon-day-two-ai-km-the-hour-human-capital/
5. **The in-house frameworks (CLOC Core 12 and ACC Legal Operations Maturity Model 2.0) still define KM as human reuse of work product and do not mention AI; ACC's 'advanced' stage requires only one full-time KM professional.** [not fact-checked; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   CLOC: 'Implement processes and standards to collect, structure, and organize knowledge to save time and improve outcomes.' ACC (PDF read directly) defines KM as capturing structured and tacit knowledge assets; early stage: 'may be getting advice on same topic repeatedly; no effort to consolidate legal work product so it can be repurposed'; advanced stage: 'Mechanisms and tools for curation' and 'At least one full-time dedicated KM professional'. The absence of AI in both is my observation from reading them, and is an opening for the article.
   Quote: "The process of capturing, distributing, and effectively using both structured and tacit knowledge assets, from work products (such as legal memos) to understanding of an issue due to prior experience."
   https://www.acc.com/sites/default/files/program-materials/upload/Legal-Opertaitons-Maturity-Model-2.0---ACC.pdf
6. **Corporate legal departments now use generative AI more than law firms (47% vs 41% in 2026, up from 23% and 28%), and 94% of professionals demand AI grounded in authoritative content.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; 2026-07-02]
   Thomson Reuters blog (Marjorie Richter) summarizing the 2026 AI in Professional Services Report and 2026 Future of Professionals Report. Also: 96% require confidential-data safeguards; 78% of corporate clients say AI-enabled quality improvements are essential but only 6% say providers deliver. Emerging roles expected: AI-specialist professionals 39%, AI implementation managers 33%, AI-specialist trainers 32%.
   https://legal.thomsonreuters.com/blog/how-ai-is-transforming-the-legal-profession/
   FACT-CHECK: The emerging-roles figures (AI-specialist professionals 39%, AI implementation managers 33%, AI-specialist trainers 32%; list also has IT and cybersecurity specialists 37% and 35%) sit in the section 'What is the impact on future roles?', which is framed around the 2024 Future of Professionals Report, not the 2026 reports. Do not present them as 2026 data. Raw page check. Confirmed: 'The 2026 AI in Professional Services Report found that 41% of law firms and 47% of corporate legal departments say their legal teams are using GenAI, up from 28% and 23%, respectively in 2025.' Confirmed: '96% demand safeguards for confidential data / 94% demand grounded outputs in authoritative content / 90% say AI must produce reasoning that can be explained and defended'. Confirmed with fuller wording: '78% of corporate clients say AI-enabled quality improvements are very important or essential, yet only 6% say most of their providers actually deliver it' (2026 Future of Professionals Report). Author Marjorie Richter, dated July 2, 2026. Page H1 is 'How AI is reshaping the legal profession'; the cited title is the meta title.
7. **iManage's 2026 benchmark found 85% of organizations piloting or using AI but only 17% embedding it in daily operations, attributing the gap to knowledge foundations.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; vendor; 2026-02-11]
   n=3,185 decision-makers, 26 countries, fielded Sep-Oct 2025, across legal, accounting, financial services (not legal-only). Organizations with mature knowledge foundations were nearly twice as likely to report revenue growth; 72% plan new document/knowledge platform investment within two years; 30% had policy-impacting incidents from unsanctioned AI; 37 minutes a day spent searching. Vendor research from a DMS provider with an interest in the conclusion.
   Quote: "AI success isn't about who experiments fastest—it's about who has done the foundational work."
   https://www.globenewswire.com/news-release/2026/02/11/3236445/0/en/organizations-with-strong-knowledge-foundations-pull-ahead-on-ai-growth-and-client-trust-new-imanage-study-finds.html
   FACT-CHECK: The 30% figure is misattributed. Release says: 'Nearly one-third of organizations report experiencing a policy-impacting incident related to unregulated AI tools, and almost 30% have delayed AI adoption due to security concerns.' So incidents = 'nearly one-third' (tools described as 'unregulated', not 'unsanctioned'); 'almost 30%' = delayed AI adoption over security concerns. Release text read raw (GlobeNewswire, Feb. 11, 2026). Confirmed: 3,185 business and technology decision-makers, 26 countries, legal / accounting and tax / financial services / asset management, conducted September-October 2025; '85% of firms are piloting, implementing, or using AI, just 17% have embedded AI into daily operations'; 'nearly twice as likely as less mature peers to report year-over-year revenue growth'; '72% ... plan to invest in a new document or knowledge management platform within the next two years'; 'an average of 37 minutes per day searching for information'. Wenzel quote confirmed (Laura Wenzel, Global Insights Director) but punctuated with a spaced hyphen, not an em dash: 'AI success isn't about who experiments fastest - it's about who has done the foundational work'.
8. **Gartner predicts organizations will abandon 60% of AI projects unsupported by AI-ready data through 2026; this is the closest available statistic for AI blocked by data quality, and it is cross-industry, not legal.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; primary; 2025-02-26]
   Same release: 63% of organizations do not have or are unsure they have the right data management practices for AI (Q3 2024 survey of 248 data management leaders). The Gartner page returned HTTP 403, so figures were read from the search-result snippet of the primary URL. No legal-specific equivalent was found.
   https://www.gartner.com/en/newsroom/press-releases/2025-02-26-lack-of-ai-ready-data-puts-ai-projects-at-risk
   FACT-CHECK: Survey detail is wrong. The release says 'A survey of 1,203 data management leaders in July 2024', not a Q3 2024 survey of 248. The number '248' does not appear anywhere in the release text. (July is in Q3, so the timing is compatible; the sample size is not.)
