The researcher read this from a search snippet, which is likely where the error came from. gartner.com returns 403 to direct fetch and curl; I read the full release text through a reader proxy (r.jina.ai) and string-checked it. Format is a Q&A with Roxane Edjlali, 'STAMFORD, Conn., February 26, 2025'. Exact text: 'Sixty-three percent of organizations either do not have or are unsure if they have the right data management practices for AI, according to a survey by Gartner.' and 'Gartner predicts that through 2026, organizations will abandon 60% of AI projects unsupported by AI-ready data.' Cross-industry, not legal -- as the finding says.
9. **RAG over curated legal content reduces but does not eliminate hallucination: leading legal research tools hallucinated 17-33% of the time, and curated, verified sources are the main mitigating factor.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2024-05]
   Stanford RegLab/HAI study (Magesh, Surani, Dahl, Suzgun, Manning, Ho), arXiv May 2024 (outside the 2025-26 window; background). A 2026-02-19 literature review by Rebecca Fordon (AI Law Librarians) adds that general models hallucinated 58-88% on legal questions and that legal platforms do better because they search 'curated, verified legal databases with citators', yet 'roughly one in five responses contains errors'.
   Quote: "Certain legal research providers have touted methods such as retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) as 'eliminating' or 'avoid[ing]' hallucinations"
   https://reglab.stanford.edu/publications/hallucination-free-assessing-the-reliability-of-leading-ai-legal-research-tools/
10. **Finished legal work product is already being repurposed as AI evaluation material: the Vals Legal AI Report benchmark dataset was built from law firms' own questions, reference documents, ideal responses and correctness criteria.** [not fact-checked; confidence high; primary; 2025-02-27]
   Reed Smith, Fisher Phillips, McDermott Will & Emery, Ogletree Deakins and four anonymous firms contributed 500+ samples. This is concrete support for the thesis that knowledge now serves as gold-standard and evaluation material for AI, not only as a precedent for humans.
   Quote: "The Consortium Firms together created the dataset used for the study and provided additional guidance and input that helped ensure the study best reflected real-world law firm work."
   https://www.vals.ai/industry-reports/vlair-2-27-25
11. **The in-house playbook has become machine-readable configuration for AI agents: Anthropic's legal plugin (launched 2026-02-03) runs contract review against the organization's own playbook, and commentators see proprietary knowledge as the remaining differentiator.** [not fact-checked; confidence high; trade-press; 2026-02-03]
   Product page: the plugin is 'configurable to your organization's playbook'; the playbook is set 'in a local settings file to define standard positions, acceptable ranges, and escalation triggers.' Bob Ambrogi (LawSites, 2026-02-03) says incumbents' defence is 'proprietary datasets and subject-matter expertise'. Legal.io (2026-03-06) reports Thomson Reuters fell 18%, RELX 14%, Wolters Kluwer 13% after launch, and says for legal ops 'the near-term priority is governance rather than product evaluation.'
   Quote: "the potent and powerful combination of proprietary datasets and subject-matter expertise"
   https://www.lawnext.com/2026/02/anthropics-legal-plugin-for-claude-cowork-may-be-the-opening-salvo-in-a-competition-between-foundation-models-and-legal-tech-incumbents.html
12. **Practitioners say the definition of a knowledge repository has widened beyond curated know-how to transactional and third-party content, and that AI magnifies data problems at scale.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; vendor; 2025-12-19]
   Tiger Eye's 2026 KM trends piece quotes Tom Humberstone (Womble Bond Dickinson): 'GenAI is all about high quality knowledge to deliver value' and that repositories now 'encompass a broader set of sources'; Rob Taylor (Tiger Eye): 'AI will magnify any data issues you may have, and do so at pace, and at scale.'; Jenni Tellyn (3Kites) on need-to-know security: 'you have to work even harder at your knowledge management'. Also reports firms appointing senior knowledge leaders before buying technology. Legal IT Insider (2026-01-15) adds Jus Mundi's CEO: 'Firms with the best knowledge management will power the best AI skills.'
   Quote: "knowledge repositories have evolved beyond traditional, curated know-how to encompass a broader set of sources now."
   https://www.tigereyeconsulting.com/legal-knowledge-management-trends-2026/
13. **85% of corporate legal departments now have a dedicated AI resource or committee, but CLOC and Harbor frame legal ops' AI role as governance and operating model; neither explicitly assigns legal ops the knowledge-curator role.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-03-02]
   Harbor/CLOC 2025 Law Department Survey (135 departments, median revenue USD 13bn; released 2025-12-08) and CLOC 2026 State of the Industry (2026-03-02): technology strategy a focus for 80%; only 32% expect lawyer headcount growth. No KM-specific metric is published. CLOC CEO Oyango Snell (2026-03-10) lists knowledge management among workflows where AI is being embedded.
   Quote: "Legal operations has become the strategic lever that allows departments to close this productivity gap."
   https://cloc.org/newsdesk/cloc-releases-2026-state-of-the-industry-report-rising-legal-demand-outpaces-budget-and-staffing-growth-forcing-operational-shift/
14. **Privilege, ethical walls and retention are live risks when knowledge is opened to AI: US v. Heppner held consumer-AI exchanges unprivileged; vector embeddings can strip DMS permission metadata; AI notetaker transcripts widen discovery and legal-hold exposure.** [not fact-checked; confidence high; other; 2026-02-11]
   Heppner (S.D.N.Y., Rakoff J., bench ruling 2026-02-10, opinion 2026-02-17); Debevoise advises enterprise tools with confidentiality terms and documenting counsel's direction. Hintyr (vendor blog, 2026-03-27): 'Vector embeddings strip away the permission metadata that traditional document management systems rely on', citing CrowdStrike and a January 2026 Microsoft Copilot sensitivity-label advisory (not independently verified). Mayer Brown (2026-06-03) on notetakers: records 'may be accessed by individuals who were not present'.
   Quote: "Because Claude is not an attorney…that alone disposes of Heppner's claim of privilege."
   https://www.debevoisedatablog.com/2026/02/11/district-court-rules-ai-generated-documents-are-not-protected-by-privilege/
15. **A sceptical counter-view holds that firms are buying AI without being able to measure impact, and that consultancy and vendor research in this area is methodologically opaque.** [not fact-checked; confidence medium; trade-press; 2026-08-25]
   Stephen Embry (Above the Law, 2026-08-25) reviews Harbor's 2026 legal lab findings: law-firm technology spend up 41% from 2021-2025 to 5-6% of revenue, yet no firm had a mature impact-measurement framework. Embry questions sample size, participants and data sources. Relevant caution: most KM-and-AI statistics located in this research originate from DMS/KM vendors or consultancies.
   Quote: "No participating firm in Harbor's 2026 legal lab reported having a mature framework for measuring AI's business impact."
   https://abovethelaw.com/2026/08/law-firms-are-buying-ai-but-are-they-really-using-it/

Viewpoints:
- KM-as-foundation (dominant): AI is a commodity; curated, governed knowledge is the constraint and the differentiator (Inside Practice, Benamram, LexisNexis, iManage, Harvey, Tiger Eye, ClearPeople).
- Context-over-content: what matters is situational context delivered to the model ('context engineering'), not the size of the precedent library (Weinberg of Harvey, Walters of Clio).
- Sceptical/measurement view: firms and departments are buying AI but cannot show impact, and vendor or consultancy 'knowledge foundation' research is self-interested and methodologically thin (Embry, Above the Law).
- Proprietary-data-moat view: incumbents' datasets and subject-matter expertise defend them against foundation-model entrants (Ambrogi), versus the market's reaction that the moat is weaker than assumed (publisher share-price falls after Anthropic's legal plugin).
- Security-first view: opening knowledge to AI collides with ethical walls, privilege, need-to-know and retention, so KM must work harder rather than looser (Tellyn, Humberstone, Hintyr, Mayer Brown, Debevoise).
- In-house governance view: legal operations' near-term AI job is governance and operating model (CLOC, Harbor, Legal.io); the in-house frameworks (Core 12, ACC) have not re-described KM for AI, and nobody yet names legal ops as knowledge curator.
- RAG-is-not-enough view: even curated RAG legal tools err roughly one time in five, so human verification and evaluation sets persist (Stanford RegLab, AI Law Librarians).

Gaps:
- ILTACON 2026 (August 2026) KM sessions and recaps: the session's web-search budget was exhausted before this search could run; nothing captured.
- ILTA KM-specific survey data (KM headcount, KM budgets, KM teams leading AI programs): the ILTA 2025 Technology Survey executive summary contains no KM breakout; full results are members-only. No ILTA KM community or KM survey publication was reached.
- No legal-specific statistic found for the share of AI projects blocked by poor knowledge or data quality; only Gartner's cross-industry 60% prediction (read via snippet because gartner.com returned 403) and unsourced event-marketing claims ('Taxonomy inconsistencies are the #1 AI deployment blocker').
- No benchmark found for KM headcount, KM investment, or the proportion of KM teams formally leading AI programs at firms or in-house. The Totum Partners '20% Director KM salary increase' figure appears only on an event marketing page and was not verified.
- SKILLS survey dashboard itself (skills.law) was not accessible; findings come from a Harvey-hosted write-up and interview. Harvey's exact relationship to the survey was not confirmed.
- No source found in which CLOC, ACC or another body explicitly assigns legal operations the role of curating the department's knowledge for AI; this remains the article's own argument.
- New knowledge sources: no neutral, citable source found describing billing narratives, decision logs and approvals, outside-counsel work product, intake Q&A, or expert corrections of AI output being managed as KM assets; only vendor marketing on redline-to-playbook conversion.
- No 2025-26 primary source with data on 'legal knowledge engineer' or 'knowledge engineer' roles; the ABA Law Technology Today piece on legal engineers returned 403.
- No evidence found of law departments or firms fine-tuning models on their own KM corpus as a mainstream practice.
- Thomson Reuters Institute KM-specific commentary, Wolters Kluwer KM-specific findings, and any 3 Geeks and a Law Blog or LawNext 2025-26 KM-specific feature were not located. Wolters Kluwer, ACC resource library, ABA, Global Legal Post, White & Case and Clio pages blocked automated fetch; Harbor pages rendered empty.
- ACC Legal Operations Maturity Model 2.0 PDF carries no publication date; release year not confirmed in this session.
- Inside Practice article by Kevin Klein displays 2025-04-16 but cites a 'PwC 2026' study, so its true date is uncertain.
- All quotes were returned through a summarizing fetch tool and should be re-verified verbatim against the live pages before publication. The Microsoft Copilot advisory (CW1226324) and CrowdStrike RAG research cited by Hintyr were not independently verified.

Fact-checker overall: I checked all 15 findings against their sources: 10 are confirmed and 5 need a correction; none are refuted or unverified. Every quote in the findings matched its source word for word, except for small truncations noted below. The five corrections concern numbers, dates or where a quote comes from.

**The five corrections**
- **Gartner (finding 8):** the release says "A survey of 1,203 data management leaders in July 2024". The figure 248 does not appear in it. The 60% and 63% figures are exact.
- **iManage (finding 7):** the 30% figure is misattributed. The release says "nearly one-third" had a policy-impacting incident from unregulated AI tools. "Almost 30%" is the share that delayed AI adoption over security concerns.
- **Thomson Reuters (finding 6):** the emerging-role figures (39%, 33%, 32%) sit in a section framed around the 2024 Future of Professionals Report, so they are not 2026 data. The 47%/41% adoption figures and the 94%, 96% and 78%/6% figures are confirmed as 2026.
- **Anthropic legal plugin (finding 11):** LawSites says the plugins were added on 30 January 2026. 3 February is the date of the LawSites post and of the share sell-off. Thomson Reuters "fell as much as 18 percent" during the session, which is an intraday figure.
- **Heppner quote (finding 14):** the quote is not on the cited Debevoise page. It is from Judge Rakoff's written memorandum filed 17 February 2026 (ECF No. 27). The ellipsis replaces "see ECF No. 23-6,". Harvard Law Review Blog also carries the quote.

**Smaller points for the writer**
- Finding 3: the cited Harvey Q&A is dated 13 March 2026. The 6 March date belongs to a companion Harvey post.
- Finding 5: the ACC Maturity Model does mention AI in other functions; only the knowledge-management function is silent on it.
- Finding 13: the CLOC quote is cut mid-sentence, and Snell's 10 March line is about Europe specifically.
- Finding 15: the "No participating firm" sentence is Harbor's own text, reproduced by Embry, so attribute it to Harbor.
- Finding 9: the 58–88% range comes from the separate "Large Legal Fictions" study, and "one in five" is Fordon's reading of the Vals accuracy scores.

**How far to trust the checks**
- The web search budget for the session was already used up, so I worked only from the cited URLs, the researcher's own notes, CourtListener and a reader proxy for Gartner, which blocks direct fetches.
- The page-fetch tool twice altered quotes it was asked to give verbatim, so I re-checked every quote against the raw page text.
- Above the Law blocked raw download; I read it through the fetch tool only, twice, with consistent results.
- The Heppner PDF is scanned, so I confirmed the quote from CourtListener's text of the filing and the Harvard Law Review Blog, not the PDF itself.
- I did not read the underlying gated reports (Harbor, CLOC, the iManage benchmark, the Thomson Reuters reports or the SKILLS dashboard). Those figures are confirmed only as far as the issuers' own press releases and blog posts state them.

**Files:** the verification notes are at C:/Users/RALPHS~1/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--code-files-spaarke-website/aa6b682a-4844-485c-99e0-cb2228b928c3/scratchpad/research/knowledge-management-and-ai.verified.md, and the raw page captures are in scratchpad/research/kmv/. No file in any git repository was modified.


## research:bi-analytics-stats

Evidence supports a "mature on spend, immature on service and value" picture. Spend analytics is established: Thomson Reuters' 2025 LDO Index (n=128, July 2025) shows 60% have e-billing/spend management and 62% routinely report spend by law firm, but only 37% have a dedicated legal BI tool (more rate it underutilized than valuable) and under 20% track cycle time or outcome quality, even though 47% of GCs prioritize service enhancement. The change drivers are structural: 84% of CLOs report to the CEO and 79% almost always attend board meetings (ACC 2026, n=1,049); TR's 2026 report (2,300+ GC interviews) finds an 86% vs 17% GC/C-suite "visibility gap" and tells GCs to institute metrics that translate to the business. ACC/MLA 2026 benchmarking (n=576, fielded Feb-Apr 2026) shows legal tech shifting to shared enterprise funding (43-45% at $5B+ companies). CLOC's 2026 State of the Industry (135 departments) puts technology strategy (80%) and financial management (72%) atop legal ops priorities amid flat budgets. On agentic use of legal data, Gartner (May 2026, secondary) predicts 60% of departments will use AI intake answering half of requests without humans by 2029; May 2026 vendor launches (Wolters Kluwer, Checkbox, Streamline AI, Mitratech, Legal.io) show agents acting on invoice, matter, and intake data, with "answers, not dashboards" messaging. Cautions: Axiom says 83% cannot measure AI value. Major gaps: no independent intake/deflection benchmarks; Gartner, ACC HTML, and WK pages were blocked; search budget ran out.

1. **Spend metrics dominate legal department reporting; service, cycle-time, and outcome metrics are tracked by fewer than one in five departments.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2025-09-24]
   TR 2025 LDO Index, n=128 US legal department professionals and GCs, fielded July 2025, published 2025-09-24 (analysis article 2025-10-08). Routinely reported: total spend by law firm 62%, spend by matter type 50%, matters opened/closed 37%, legal spend to revenue 21%, quality of legal outcomes 18%; cycle time, costs avoided, AI usage, savings from legal tech (8%) all under 15%. Meanwhile 47% of GCs say they are more focused on service enhancement than cost reduction (only 7% prioritize cost). Figures read from the full PDF; some mid-chart label alignment is ambiguous.
   Quote: "corporate legal departments are tracking metrics related nearly exclusively to cost and to a lesser extent, efficiency."
   https://www.thomsonreuters.com/en-us/posts/wp-content/uploads/sites/20/2025/09/Legal-Department-Operations-Index-2025.pdf
2. **60% of departments have e-billing/spend management, but only 37% have a dedicated legal business intelligence solution, and more rate it underutilized than valuable.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2025-09-24]
   TR 2025 LDO Index Figure 16 (n=128, July 2025). Legal BI: 16% 'valuable' + 21% 'underutilized' = 37% have it; 19% looking to procure within 24 months; 33% 'not important'. E-billing/spend management: 42% + 18% = 60%. Legal workflow automation: 13% + 25% = 38%, 25% looking to procure. 63% of respondents with legal ops backgrounds use data analytics to find cost savings vs 29% of GCs. This cuts against a simple 'most departments have BI' framing: spend reporting is mature, dedicated BI is not, and utilization is the constraint.
   https://www.thomsonreuters.com/en-us/posts/wp-content/uploads/sites/20/2025/09/Legal-Department-Operations-Index-2025.pdf
3. **There is an 86% vs 17% 'visibility gap' between GCs and the C-suite on legal's contribution, and TR's prescription is business-language metrics.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; 2026-03-24]
   2026 State of the Corporate Law Department, published 2026-03-24, drawn from 'more than 2,300 interviews with corporate general counsel' (TR Legal blog says 2,400+); field dates not stated; C-suite survey sample not given. 42% of C-suite say legal contributes little or nothing. Technology as a strategic priority doubled from 14% to 28%; 47% have GenAI access. 'Decision-making using analytics' is an AI benefit already felt by 19%, anticipated within 12 months by 39%. TR advises presenting legal spend as a percentage of revenue and calculating internal matter cycle time the way it is applied to outside counsel.
   Quote: "GCs need to institute metrics for success that translate to the rest of the business."
   https://www.thomsonreuters.com/en/institute/reports/state-of-the-corporate-law-department-report-2026
   FACT-CHECK: The 47% GenAI-access figure is cited by the report from TR Institute's 2026 AI in Professional Services Report (Feb 2026), not from the GC interviews. The 'Decision-making using analytics' 19% felt / 39% anticipated figures (Figure 15) are, per the report's own text, 'data from the Thomson Reuters Future of Professionals Report 2025' (June 2025), reproduced in the 2026 report. They are not findings of the 2,300 GC interviews and date from 2025. Headline confirmed in the landing page (dated March 24, 2026) and the full PDF: 86% of GCs vs 17% of C-suite; 42% 'A little/Not at all'; TR uses the term 'visibility gap'; 'more than 2,300 interviews with corporate general counsel'; TR Legal blog (2026-06-16) says 'more than 2,400 GCs worldwide'. Technology priority 28% vs 14% confirmed. Quote is in the PDF ('This also means that GCs need to institute metrics for success that translate to the rest of the business.'). Percentage-of-revenue and internal matter-cycle-time advice both confirmed. Two sub-statistics are mis-sourced in the finding, see corrected detail.
4. **The audience for legal information has structurally expanded: 84% of CLOs report to the CEO and 79% almost always attend board meetings.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-01]
   2026 ACC Chief Legal Officers Survey Key Findings (PDF path dated 2026-01), 1,049 CLOs, 20 industries, 43 countries; field dates not given in key findings. CLOs have majority oversight of compliance (64%) and corporate secretary (62%); 74% provide proactive strategic counsel; operational efficiency is the top strategic initiative (53%); budget/resource constraints top barrier (35%); 36% in active GenAI deployment; 47% say their CEO wants them to develop technology/AI proficiency.
   Quote: "legal expertise is now integrated into business planning during the inception of projects rather than at the point of crisis."
   https://www.acc.com/sites/default/files/2026-01/2026-ACC-Chief-Legal-Officers-Survey-Key-Findings.pdf
5. **Legal technology is moving from a departmental expense to a shared enterprise resource, especially at large companies.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-06]
   ACC/MLA 2026 Law Department Management Benchmarking Report: 576 legal departments, 45 countries, fielded 2026-02-11 to 2026-04-17, published June 2026. 53% fund legal tech entirely within legal; shared-services funding 29% (25% in 2024); no tech budget 18%. At $5B-$20B companies shared funding rose to 43% from 29% in 2024; 45% at $20B+. Median legal tech spend is 3% of total legal spend (US$130K median; US$2.5M at $20B+). Legal spend/revenue hit a six-year low of 0.43%; 367 employees per lawyer; 8 lawyers per legal ops professional; legal ops about 5% of staff since 2022. Report has no BI/dashboard/intake questions.
   Quote: "as revenue grows, the legal department's technology stack shifts from a siloed departmental expense to a shared enterprise resource."
   https://www.acc.com/sites/default/files/2026-06/2026-ACC-Law-Department-Management-Benchmarking-Report.pdf
6. **CLOC's 2026 State of the Industry shows technology strategy and financial management as the top legal ops priorities amid a 'structural productivity gap'.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-03-02]
   CLOC press release 2026-03-02; based on the 2025 Harbor Law Department Survey (published 2025-12-08): 135 law departments, 15+ industries, median revenue US$13B; field dates not published. Technology strategy 80%, financial management 72%, outside counsel/vendor management 62%; 85% have a dedicated AI resource or committee; only 37% expect outside counsel spend to rise (58% prior year), 47% inside spend (65% prior), 32% lawyer headcount. Public materials contain no analytics, dashboard, BI, or intake statistics; full report is member-gated.
   Quote: "Demand is accelerating in areas like regulatory compliance and cybersecurity, yet budget and staffing growth are not keeping pace."
   https://cloc.org/newsdesk/cloc-releases-2026-state-of-the-industry-report-rising-legal-demand-outpaces-budget-and-staffing-growth-forcing-operational-shift/
7. **ACC's own definition of 'Advanced' metrics maturity is self-service dashboards plus predictive analytics; data integrity is the named early-stage hurdle.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; primary; unknown]
   ACC Legal Operations Maturity Model 2.0, Metrics & Analytics function. Early: manual tracking, 'Uneven integrity/cleanliness of data'. Intermediate: 'Most metrics generated through manual synthesis of data from disparate systems'. Advanced: leading and lagging indicators against baselines and targets, central data source, predictive analytics, annual pruning of metrics that do not support decisions. The PDF carries no publication date (acc.com HTML pages returned 403), so version date is unverified. Useful as the baseline the market is now moving beyond.
   Quote: "Department leaders directly access dashboards to filter data and answer questions to derive insights and make better and faster operational, financial, and legal strategy decisions"
   https://www.acc.com/sites/default/files/program-materials/upload/Legal-Opertaitons-Maturity-Model-2.0---ACC.pdf
8. **Gartner predicts AI-driven intake will capture all requests at 60% of legal departments and answer half without human intervention by 2029; legal tech budgets double by 2028.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; trade-press; 2026-06-09]
   Gartner press release dated 2026-05-26 (gartner.com returned 403); details taken from Lawyers Weekly coverage by Grace Robbie, 2026-06-09, quoting Weston Wicks, Senior Director Analyst. Also: by 2029 about 50% of contract reviews delegated to self-service systems escalating one in ten. These are analyst forecasts, not survey measurements. A separate Gartner release (2026-07-15, via Lawyers Weekly 2026-07-28) says 56% of legal departments anticipate a headcount freeze or reduction in 2026 (sample not stated).
   Quote: "we predict that 60 per cent of legal departments will use AI-driven intake systems that capture all requests and answer one-half of those without human intervention"
   https://www.lawyersweekly.com.au/biglaw/44448-legal-tech-spending-set-to-double-by-2028-amid-ai-boom
9. **Vendors are explicitly positioning natural-language answers embedded in workflow as the successor to dashboards.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; vendor; 2026-05-11]
   Legal.io press release, 2026-05-11 (CLOC Global Institute): AI assistant answering natural-language questions over rate, panel, spend, and matter data. COO Hannah Konitshek: 'Legal operations has matured into a discipline that runs on data, but most teams are still stitching that data together manually.' Parallel moves: Mitratech ARIES (2026-05-11) answers natural-language questions on outside counsel performance and tracks spend vs budget in matter views; Wolters Kluwer BillAnalyzer (2026-05-05) adds natural-language interrogation of invoices. Vendor source.
   Quote: "In-house teams don't need another dashboard. They need answers."
   https://globalinstitute.cloc.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/Legal.io-Press-Release.pdf
10. **Legal data is now an input to agents that act, not only to reports: agentic invoice adjustment, conversation-to-matter intake, and AI-maintained matter status.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; vendor; 2026-05-05]
   Wolters Kluwer LegalVIEW BillAnalyzer Invoice Review Agent (2026-05-05) 'identifies non-compliant invoice line items and automatically implements adjustments', auditable and reversible, trained on 'more than $200 billion in actual legal invoice data'. Checkbox (2026-05-11) AI Agent Actions create populated matters and start workflows from a conversation. Streamline AI (2026-05-11) launched 'a system of interconnected AI agents' from intake to execution; CEO Kathy Zhu: moving 'beyond systems that track work and toward a platform that organizes, manages, and executes it.' Trade press (Legal IT Insider, 2026-05-15) confirms embedded intelligence and workflow automation as CGI 2026's dominant theme. Vendor sources.
   Quote: "Future-ready legal teams know that it isn't enough to simply flag non-compliance"
   https://globalinstitute.cloc.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/Wolters-Kluwer-Press-Release.pdf
11. **Cycle time is being reframed as the key intake metric, and its historical weakness is a data-quality problem (humans updating matter status).** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; vendor; 2026-05-11]
   Checkbox press release 2026-05-11: 'Intelligent Status Update' sets matter status from email/Slack activity under plain-English rules so reporting separates 'time spent by legal from time spent waiting on the business' and supports SLA reporting. Routing model: request is 'answered instantly, escalated into a process, or routed to the right attorney'. Independent corroboration of the gap: TR LDO 2025 shows cycle time is routinely reported by under 20% of departments. No independent benchmark of request volumes, triage, or deflection rates was found; Streamline AI's benchmark claims (e.g. 93.7% of teams have no self-service knowledge base) are undated with undisclosed methodology.
   Quote: "Cycle time is one of the most important metrics for legal as it helps to reveal where delays are coming from and proves that the bottleneck is often the business, not legal."
   https://globalinstitute.cloc.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/05/Checkbox-Press-Release.pdf
12. **Measurement of AI value is the new metrics frontier: 83% of in-house teams cannot measure whether AI spend is working and only 7% have scaled AI.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; vendor; 2026-06-29]
   Axiom 2026 In-House Legal AI Report, published 2026-06-29; n=528 in-house legal leaders, six countries, fielded March 2026 by InsightDynamo. ALSP vendor-sponsored. Consistent with TR 2026 landing-page statement that 'very few are collecting success metrics around AI's implementation', Blickstein's 18th LDO survey (about 68-70 companies; only 23% 'fully operational' with AI; usability overtook security as top AI concern), and RLLB 2026's 'AI ROI Playbook' workshop. FTI/Relativity GC Report 2026 (224-respondent survey summer 2025 plus 30 interviews Sept 2025; release 2026-03-11): GenAI use 87% vs 44% prior year; formal tech roadmap 53% vs 25%.
   https://www.axiomlaw.com/resources/articles/legal-ai-survey-report
13. **Agentic workflows are reaching production in legal, with stale data already causing incidents; self-assessed readiness far exceeds tested readiness.** [UNVERIFIED; confidence low; other; 2026-06-17]
   The Legal Stack, Agentic Deployment Readiness Report 2026, published 2026-06-17; n=200 legal ops and IT leaders fielded March-April 2026, but only 55 are Fortune 1000 in-house departments. 38% overall have at least one agentic workflow in production (47% of the in-house subgroup, roughly 26 departments); legal operations leads by function at 54%. 58% call themselves 'agentic ready' vs 11% meeting all four test criteria; 22% of production deployers report an agent acting on outdated information (e.g. invoice processed after dispute resolved: 11% of e-billing users). Publisher is unfamiliar and self-described as independent; verify before citing. For comparison, ILTA's 2026 Technology Survey (508 law firms, released about 2026-09-14) found 13% already using agentic AI.
   Quote: "22% of respondents with production deployments report at least one incident involving an agent acting on outdated information"
   https://www.thelegalstack.org/research/the-legal-ai-agentic-deployment-readiness-report-2026-how
   FACT-CHECK:  The page does say what the finding reports (June 17, 2026; n=200 = AmLaw 200 n=84, regional firms n=61, Fortune 1000 in-house n=55; 38%/47%/54%; 58% vs 11%; 22% stale-data incidents; 11% of e-billing users). I cannot treat it as credible. The About page names no people, no organisation and no location, publishes without bylines, and states that AI tools are used for research and drafting. The stated 'margin of error ±4.8% at 95% confidence' is arithmetically inconsistent with n=200 (which gives ±6.9%; ±4.8% needs about n=417). No independent corroboration found. Recommend not citing. The ILTA comparison is solid: eDiscovery Today Sept 14, 2026, '508 firms', '13% of respondents said they are already using Agentic AI on the business side or in practice' (law firms, not in-house).
14. **CFO-legal budgeting is shifting toward value-based models, with a perception gap over who controls the legal budget.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; vendor; 2025-09-08]
   Axiom 2026 In-House Legal Budgeting Survey Report, published 2025-09-08; 500+ respondents including GCs, CLOs, deputy GCs, and CFOs across eight countries; field dates not stated. 49% changed budgeting models in the past 12 months and 36% plan changes in 2026; 89% of legal leaders rate the CFO relationship excellent but there is an '18-point authority gap' (search snippet: 50% of CFOs say they control legal budget-setting vs 32% of legal leaders who say so); 78% mandated to implement AI without dedicated budget. Vendor-sponsored.
   https://www.axiomlaw.com/resources/articles/legal-budgeting-survey-report-2026
   FACT-CHECK: The 50% of CFOs vs 32% of legal leaders breakdown behind the '18-point authority gap' does not appear on the public page and was not seen; the arithmetic fits but it rests on a search snippet. The interpretation that the gap concerns 'who controls legal budget-setting' is likewise not stated on the public page. Print only the 18-point figure unless the gated report is obtained. Axiom page confirms: released September 8, 2025; 500+ GCs, CLOs, deputy GCs and CFOs; eight countries; '49% of legal departments transformed their budgeting models in the past 12 months, with another 36% planning changes'; '78% are mandated to implement AI without dedicated budgets'; '89% of legal leaders rate CFO relationships as excellent, but a 18-point authority gap undermines strategic collaboration'. Field dates not stated. Vendor research.
15. **Conference facts: CLOC Global Institute ran May 11-14, 2026 in Chicago and launched the Compass maturity platform; ILTACON 2026 ran Aug 23-27 in Nashville; RLLB 2026 ran Sept 8-11 in Las Vegas.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-05-12]
   CGI 2026: 2,300+ attendees (CLOC news hub); Compass launched 2026-05-12 with Neota Logic as an interactive companion to the Core 12 Maturity Assessment Playbook (four stages; beta, members only). A vendor recap (Steno, 2026-06-03) says Compass plots Business Intelligence, Financial Management, Firm & Vendor Management, and Information Governance across Reactive/Emerging/Developing/Leading; CLOC's release does not confirm that area list. Same recap reports Meta's Mike Haven using 'Deflection' as the first of four AI value pillars. ILTACON: Gaylord Opryland, 80+ sessions. RLLB: Fontainebleau Las Vegas, 900+ attendees, 100+ sessions, includes 'The AI ROI Playbook for Legal Teams'. No session-level data/analytics recaps were retrievable for ILTACON or RLLB.
   Quote: "Legal departments across industries told us the same thing: more work, tighter budgets, flat headcount."
   https://cloc.org/newsdesk/cloc-launches-compass-an-interactive-platform-to-advance-legal-operations-maturity-in-partnership-with-neota-logic/

Viewpoints:
- Thomson Reuters Institute (State of the Corporate Law Department 2026): capability is no longer the issue, visibility is. Departments meet their own internal metrics but the C-suite does not see it (86% vs 17%); metrics must be restated in business-outcome language, and efficiency-only reporting risks inviting further cuts.
- Thomson Reuters LDO Index 2025: measurement is mature on spend and immature on service, outcomes, cycle time, and technology value; GCs say they prioritize service enhancement yet still measure cost; many owned tools (including legal BI) are underutilized.
- CLOC / Harbor: a structural productivity gap (rising regulatory and cyber demand, flat budgets and headcount) makes technology strategy, financial management, and governance the primary levers; maturity assessment itself is being productized (Compass). CLOC's CEO says the AI conversation has matured to what worked, what broke, and how it is governed.
- ACC: the CLO is now a CEO-reporting, board-attending enterprise hub for non-financial risk; benchmarking is framed as 'performance storytelling' in the language of the business; legal tech is becoming a shared enterprise resource co-funded with IT/procurement.
- Gartner (via secondary coverage): multi-agent legal applications and AI-driven intake will deflect roughly half of requests by 2029, legal tech budgets will double by 2028, and most departments face headcount freezes, so investment must demonstrate business value.
- Vendors (Legal.io, Mitratech, Wolters Kluwer, Checkbox, Streamline AI): dashboards are giving way to natural-language answers embedded in workflow and to agents that act on legal data (adjust invoices, create matters, maintain status, triage requests). 'In-house teams don't need another dashboard. They need answers.'
- Cautionary voices (Axiom, Blickstein, The Legal Stack): AI adoption is near-universal but few can measure its value (83% cannot), few have scaled (7%) or are 'fully operational' (23%), self-assessed agent readiness far exceeds tested readiness, and stale data is already causing agent errors.

Gaps:
- WebSearch budget for the session was exhausted after 30 searches; EY, Deloitte, KPMG, and other GC studies were not checked, and no further discovery searches were possible for ILTACON/RLLB recaps or vendor reports.
- CLOC 2026 State of the Industry: no public figures on analytics, BI, dashboards, metrics programs, or intake; full report is member-gated. Field dates for the underlying 2025 Harbor survey are not published.
- ACC 2025 Law Department Management Benchmarking Report not reviewed (only the 2026 edition). Full 2026 ACC CLO Survey (beyond the Key Findings PDF) not reviewed; its field dates were not found. ACC benchmarking has no BI/dashboard/intake questions.
- Thomson Reuters 2026 Legal Department Operations Index: no 2026 edition found as of 2026-09-21 (2025 edition published 2025-09-24). TR's C-suite survey sample size behind the 17% figure was not found.
- Gartner: all gartner.com pages returned 403; statistics come from secondary trade press or search snippets. No current, verifiable Gartner statistic on legal dashboard/analytics maturity; the 'nearly 60% descriptive / 16% diagnostic analytics' figure is undated and should not be presented as current.
- Blickstein Group Law Department Operations Survey: full report not accessible; no metrics/dashboard statistics retrieved; sample size conflicts (68 companies per Wolters Kluwer summary vs 'more than 70' on Blickstein's site); field dates unknown.
- Legal front door / intake: no independent benchmark of request volumes, cycle times, triage accuracy, or self-service deflection rates was found. Only vendor claims (Streamline AI, Checkbox) with undisclosed methodology and one Gartner forecast.
- No statistic found on audit-committee or board reporting from legal data, or on the share of departments producing executive/board dashboards; procurement and business-unit consumption of legal data is evidenced only indirectly.
- ILTACON 2026 and RLLB 2026: dates, venues, and tracks confirmed, but no session-level data/analytics recaps found. ILTA 2026 Technology Survey BI/data findings are paywalled; field dates not published; it is law-firm focused.
- Harbor 2026 Legal Department Maturity Index Survey (presented at CGI 2026): only a one-line summary found; no release URL, sample, or figures.
- Brightflag, Onit, LawVu, Mitratech: no 2026 survey-based reports found (LawVu's latest located is the 2024 In-House Legal Technology Report fielded October 2023; Onit ELR dates to 2022). Wolters Kluwer LegalVIEW Insights 2026 pages returned 403; details are from search snippets only.
- ACC Legal Operations Maturity Model 2.0 publication date could not be verified (PDF undated; acc.com HTML returned 403).
- The Legal Stack report's publisher credibility is unverified and its in-house subsample is small (n=55); treat as low confidence.

Fact-checker overall: Twelve of the 15 findings are confirmed, two are corrected and one is unverified. I read every primary source URL the researcher gave. The PDFs (TR LDO Index, TR 2026 State of the Corporate Law Department, ACC CLO key findings, ACC/MLA benchmarking, ACC Maturity Model, and five CLOC Global Institute press releases) were downloaded and text-extracted locally, so quotes and numbers were checked against the full text rather than summaries.

The session's WebSearch budget was already exhausted (200 of 200), so I could not search independently for primary sources. Verification used the given URLs, URLs in the researcher's own notes, and publisher pages. I did not route around the cap through a search engine.

Points the writer should act on:
- **Finding 3 (TR 2026 State report):** two sub-statistics are borrowed from other TR reports. The 19%/39% "decision-making using analytics" figures come from the Future of Professionals Report 2025. The 47% GenAI-access figure comes from the 2026 AI in Professional Services Report.
- **Finding 13 (The Legal Stack):** do not cite it. The publisher is anonymous, publishes without bylines, and says it drafts with AI tools. Its stated margin of error of ±4.8% is impossible for n=200, which gives ±6.9%.
- **Finding 14 (Axiom budgeting):** the 50%/32% breakdown behind the "18-point authority gap" is not on the public page. Print only the 18-point figure.
- **Finding 9 (Legal.io):** the "don't need another dashboard" quote belongs to Pieter Gunst, CEO. The finding left it unattributed.
- **Finding 8 (Gartner):** both items are confirmed only through Lawyers Weekly. The Gartner release dates of 2026-05-26 and 2026-07-15 are unverified because gartner.com returns 403.
- **Finding 4 (ACC CLO Survey):** FTI Technology's sponsorship is unverified, because acc.com HTML pages return 403 and the key-findings PDF names no sponsor.
- **Finding 5 (ACC/MLA benchmarking):** the report's copyright notice requires "Reprinted with permission" wording on extracts and prohibits uploading the report into AI tools.
- **Finding 15 (ILTACON 2026):** the Aug 23-27 dates and "80+ sessions" were seen only on a vendor blog. The Nashville venue is confirmed by ILTA's own wrap-up page.

The verification notes are at C:/Users/RALPHS~1/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--code-files-spaarke-website/aa6b682a-4844-485c-99e0-cb2228b928c3/scratchpad/research/bi-analytics-stats.verified.md, and the text extracts of the PDFs are in the fc/ subfolder beside it. No file in any git repository was modified.


## research:palantir-intelligence-platform-model

Palantir's current model (docs and an April 2026 blog) defines the platform as decision-centric, not data-centric: every operational decision is modelled as data, logic, action and security, integrated in one governed system. The semantic part maps existing sources (structured systems, documents, streams, models) into entities, attributes and relationships bound to live data. The kinetic part defines governed actions (rules, validations, approvals, notifications) and functions, with write-back to source systems. The dynamic part binds models, simulations (sandboxed scenarios) and AI. Each submitted action is recorded as data ("decision lineage", action log) and fed back for learning. Organization structure is modelled as ordinary entities and relationships (Employee, manager link, an "Assign Employee" action); security combines discretionary roles with mandatory labels that propagate with data through lineage, plus row/column policies computed at runtime, applied identically to humans and agents. Palantir explicitly contrasts this with reporting databases, "golden tables", thin semantic layers and dashboards: the differentiator is closing the action loop. Build method: start from a decision-focused use case, write requirements as user/interface/decision/inputs/action, model reality not source systems, bind rather than replace systems, define actions, build workflow apps, add AI last, validate with real questions, expand. Agents get entity queries, functions and actions as tools; by default they stage proposals for human review and earn autonomy gradually. Caveats: two key blog posts were unreachable (403); search budget ran out after 4 searches; trademark status verified only for Palantir, Gotham, Palantir Foundry. A full translation table is in the notes.

1. **The platform is defined as decision-centric: each operational decision comprises data, logic, action and security, integrated in one system.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Docs list four components: Data (information leveraged), Logic (heuristics and computational processes), Action (orchestration and execution), Security (compliance with operational policies). Same text appears in the 2026-04-28 blog 'Connecting Agents to Decisions'. This supersedes the older semantic/kinetic/dynamic framing in current documentation.
   Quote: "The Ontology represents the decisions in an enterprise, not simply the data."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/ontology/why-ontology
2. **Palantir's own line between an operational platform and an analytical one is closing the action loop.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Docs argue traditional data architectures do not capture reasoning or resulting actions, and that conventional analytics stay disconnected from operations. Integrating decision data 'requires a different architecture than a classical database management solution optimized for reporting and analytics.' The April 2026 blog adds the 'golden tables that flatten the richness of operations into narrow schemas' contrast.
   Quote: "Closing the action loop as decisions are made in real-time is what distinguishes an operational system from an analytical system."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/ontology/why-ontology
3. **Palantir explicitly rejects the label 'semantic layer' for its system.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Architecture Center page groups the system into a Language (entities, links, properties, actions, logic), an Engine (read and write architecture, change data capture) and a Toolchain (SDK, DevOps). It also states 'semantics must be paired with kinetics' and uses the nouns/verbs metaphor. Partner material (Cognizant) still calls it a semantic layer, so usage is inconsistent in the market.
   Quote: "The Ontology is not a "semantic layer"; the fourfold integration and operationalization of data, logic, action, and security cannot be accomplished with a thin semantic layer or a monolithic design."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/architecture-center/ontology-system
4. **The three-layer wording (semantic, kinetic, dynamic) comes from product marketing pages; the cleanest primary mapping is semantic = data, kinetic = action, dynamic = logic.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; primary; unknown]
   Foundry page: 'It integrates the semantic, kinetic, and dynamic elements of your business'. AIP page maps the three adjectives to data, logic and action. Docs overview lists semantic elements (objects, properties, links) and kinetic elements (actions, functions, dynamic security) and calls the result a digital twin of the organization. No primary page was found that formally defines three named layers.
   Quote: "a developer toolchain that surfaces the full power of the semantic data, dynamic logic, and kinetic action integrated into the Ontology"
   https://www.palantir.com/platforms/aip/
   FACT-CHECK: Do not cite the 'semantic data, dynamic logic, and kinetic action' quote to https://www.palantir.com/platforms/aip/ ; it is not on that page as currently published. Safe primary cites: Foundry page for the three adjectives; https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/ontology/overview for semantic vs kinetic elements. The three-way mapping is unverified. Foundry product page sentence 'It integrates the semantic, kinetic, and dynamic elements of your business' seen (rendered page). Docs overview sentence on semantic elements (objects, properties, links) and kinetic elements (actions, functions, dynamic security) as a digital twin seen verbatim. But the exact_quote attributed to the AIP page was NOT found there on 2026-09-21: 'semantic data', 'kinetic action' and 'full power' are all absent from the rendered page, and also absent from /platforms/ontology/ and /docs/foundry/aip/overview. The semantic=data / kinetic=action / dynamic=logic mapping therefore rests on a quote I could not locate.
5. **Decisions are captured as first-class data through an action log and end-to-end decision lineage, then fed back for learning.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Action log generates a record per submitted action answering 'What decision was made, by whom, when, and in what context?'. Why-ontology docs describe lineage of when a decision was made, on which data version, through which application, and reuse of aggregate decisions as training data and prompt principles. Use-case docs describe 'capturing decisions as data' as closing the operational loop.
   Quote: "The action log is designed to capture decisions made by submitting actions and make these decisions available as data in the Ontology."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/action-types/action-log
6. **Actions are governed transactions with rules, validations, side effects and write-back to source systems; the platform binds to existing systems rather than replacing them.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Action types define edits plus side effects; submission criteria encode who may act under what object state; webhooks push to Salesforce, SAP or any HTTP system, and a 'writeback' webhook runs first so failure blocks internal changes. Virtual tables mean data 'never requires data to be duplicated'. May 2026 blog: the ERP becomes 'the system of record for accountability, not the system of constraint for operations.'
   Quote: "This enables workflows in Foundry to connect directly with source systems and write back data and decisions into those systems."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/action-types/webhooks
   FACT-CHECK: 'never requires data to be duplicated' is on https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/architecture-center/multimodal-data-plane : 'MMDP allows Iceberg catalogs to be managed within Palantir, or as virtual catalogs and virtual tables that are registered from these (or other) providers. This means that leveraging data in the Ontology for operational applications and AI-driven automations never requires data to be duplicated.' ERP quote source: https://blog.palantir.com/enterprise-business-software-and-the-mixed-up-chameleon-problem-f16df333bc71 (2026-05-29). Webhooks page: Salesforce / SAP / any HTTP server sentence seen; writeback-runs-first-and-failure-blocks-changes sentence seen. Submission criteria (user and parameter/object conditions) seen. ERP quote seen verbatim in blog 'Enterprise Business Software and the Mixed-Up Chameleon Problem' dated 2026-05-29. The exact_quote sentence itself was not returned verbatim by my fetch (probable, not eyeballed). The 'never requires data to be duplicated' phrase is not on the virtual tables page.
7. **Organization structure, roles and staff assignments are modelled as ordinary entities, relationships and governed actions, not a separate module.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Docs use an Employee entity throughout: a self-referencing 'Direct Report <-> Manager' relationship, an Employee to Employer relationship, and an 'Assign Employee' action that changes role, links the new manager, notifies old and new managers, and validates that only HR may perform it. Process definitions include tasks, transitions and teams. The conclusion that there is no separate org-chart module is my inference.
   Quote: "You may create an Assign Employee action type that defines how users can change the role property value for a given Employee object."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/action-types/overview
   FACT-CHECK: Docs do not say only HR may perform the action. Wording is: 'Validate that authorized employees such as those working in human resources can perform the action.' Exact quote seen. New-manager link rule and old/new manager notification seen. 'Direct Report <-> Manager' self-link and 'Employee -> Employer' (Employee to Company) seen on https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/object-link-types/link-types-overview . 'Process definitions include tasks, transitions and teams' not verified. 'No separate org-chart module' is the researcher's own inference and should stay flagged as such.
8. **Security travels with the data through mandatory, lineage-propagated controls, layered with discretionary roles and runtime row/column policies; Palantir flags that row/column controls are read-time only.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Mandatory controls (markings, classification-based controls, organizations) propagate through derivation; roles are discretionary and location-based. Object and property security policies give row, column and cell security independent of source permissions, computed at runtime using group attributes from SSO. Docs warn these do not extend to downstream outputs and must be paired with a mandatory control. Checkpoints add purpose justification; Approvals persist as an audit log.
   Quote: "Unlike role-based access, which is based on where data lives in the platform, Markings travel with the data."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/security/markings
9. **The build method starts from a decision-focused use case and a requirement sentence of the form user type, interface, decision, decision inputs, action.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Docs: a use case is not 'integrate source system X' or 'apply ML technique Y'. Template asks for the ultimate and intermediate decisions and which users own them. Sequencing guidance: establish the data asset and guided decision-making first, then models and AI. Guidance explicitly says not to start from 'a need to build a sales dashboard'.
   Quote: "A use case is a time-bound effort by a dedicated team to support a specific decision-making process."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/getting-started/delivering-a-use-case
   FACT-CHECK: 'A use case is not "integrate source system X" or "apply ML technique Y".' is on https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/use-case-life-cycle/overview . Template '[User Type] [Interface] [Decision] [Decision Inputs] [Action]' and 'What is the ultimate decision being made? What are the intermediate decisions? Which users go with which decisions?' are on https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/use-case-life-cycle/distilling-functional-requirements . Sequencing ('data asset' and 'guided decision-making' first, model-driven workflows later) is on https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/use-case-life-cycle/sequencing-development . Exact quote and the sales-dashboard guidance are on the cited page. The other details are real but live on different pages in the use-case-life-cycle section, where a use case is defined differently ('a time-bound effort by a dedicated team to deliver new capabilities on the platform for a set of users').
10. **Entity modelling guidance: model the real-world domain, not source systems; use governed actions for human or agent decisions and pipelines for automated transformation; validate with real business questions.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Four prioritized principles: domain-driven design; do not repeat yourself; open for extension, closed for modification; composition over deep hierarchies. Named anti-patterns include mapping source columns one to one. Validation page recommends timed drills with unseen business questions for both people and agents, sourced from what leaders repeatedly ask.
   Quote: "Model reality, not systems: Object types should represent real-world entities, not individual source system or department representations."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/ontology/ontology-best-practices
11. **AI agents use the model through tools derived from it (entity queries, functions, actions), under the same permissions as humans, with proposals staged for human approval by default and autonomy widened gradually.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Agent tool types in docs: Action (run automatically or after user confirmation), Object query, Function, request clarification. Why-ontology docs: by default actions can only be staged by the AI; trusted processes may later close the loop without review; 'latitude given to AI can be expanded or contracted'. Agents are likened to a new team member granted wider purview over time. Note the product was renamed from AIP Agent Studio to AIP Chatbot Studio.
   Quote: "With the consequences of the plan safely staged in a scenario, the agent’s proposed decision is handed off to a human analyst for final review."
   https://www.palantir.com/docs/foundry/ontology/why-ontology
12. **Palantir positions grounding in the operational model as going beyond retrieval-augmented generation, combining LLM reasoning with deterministic logic tools.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2024-01-31]
   Blog series coins 'Ontology Augmented Generation (OAG)': grounding in data, logic and actions rather than documents alone. Rationale given: LLMs are weak at forecasting and optimization, so forecast models and optimizers are surfaced as tools. Docs: deterministic functions 'complement the non-deterministic reasoning of LLMs'.
   Quote: "OAG is a more expansive, decision-centric version of Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)."
   https://blog.palantir.com/building-with-palantir-aip-logic-tools-for-rag-oag-fdaf8938d02e
13. **The 2022 definition was narrower: an ontology as a mapping of data to meaning that separates pipelines from applications and embeds logic, write-back and security once.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2022-10-03]
   RFx blog lists requirements for an ontology service: separate data and application layers, dynamic metadata, object sets, object functions, object actions, real-time object storage, webhooks and write-back, and security resident in the ontology so app builders need not re-implement it. Shows the framing shifted from semantic mapping (2022) to decision-centric (2024 to 2026).
   Quote: "An ontology refers to the systematic mapping of data to meaningful semantic concepts."
   https://blog.palantir.com/ontology-finding-meaning-in-data-palantir-rfx-blog-series-1-399bd1a5971b
14. **The bootcamp delivery approach replaces months-long pilots with a build on real customer data in days; Palantir's own filing notes it may not convert to revenue.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; primary; 2026-02-17]
   Bootcamp page: 0 to use case in 5 days, with three aims (understand, develop initial use cases, onboard users). FY2025 10-K (signed 2026-02-17) describes bootcamps as shorter, more cost-effective programs than prior months-long pilots, which 'may result in no or minimal future revenue'. April 2026 blog introduces the newer name 'AIP AgentCamps' (outcomes in hours). The same 10-K states registered trademarks for Palantir, Gotham, Palantir Foundry and the logo only.
   Quote: "our use of AIP bootcamps, which allow us to deliver real workflows on actual customer data in days"
   https://investors.palantir.com/files/2025%20FY%20PLTR%2010-K.pdf
15. **A competitor critique holds that the model requires its own indexed copy of data and vendor-led specialist mapping, creating lock-in; this conflicts with Palantir's no-duplication claim.** [CORRECTED; confidence medium; vendor; 2026-07-16]
   Elementum (a competing vendor, so interested) argues self-hosting does not remove the ingestion requirement and that mapping source systems to entity types is specialized work typically led by the vendor's engineers. Palantir docs counter with virtual tables and Iceberg-based federation. Separately, an independent Medium explainer (Caruso, 2025-06-13) mis-defines the kinetic layer as data mapping and the dynamic layer as rules and permissions, showing the three-layer vocabulary is unstable outside Palantir.
   Quote: "still needs its own indexed copy of the data, regardless of whose cloud that copy sits in."
   https://www.elementum.ai/blog/palantir-ontology-explained
   FACT-CHECK: Actual page title / H1 is 'What Is Palantir's Ontology, and Where Does Your Data Actually Go?' - not 'Palantir Ontology Explained: What It Means for Your Data' (that matches only the URL slug). URL, date (July 16, 2026), author 'Elementum Team' and quote confirmed. Full sentence: 'The Ontology still needs its own indexed copy of the data, regardless of whose cloud that copy sits in.' Vendor-led mapping passage seen verbatim. Palantir's counter (virtual tables, Iceberg, 'never requires data to be duplicated') confirmed on the multimodal-data-plane docs page, though the word 'federation' does not appear there. The Caruso Medium explainer (2025-06-13) had no URL and was not checked: unverified.

Viewpoints:
- Palantir (current, 2024 to 2026): the system is decision-centric (data, logic, action, security), explicitly not a semantic layer, and is the shared foundation for human-agent teaming with staged autonomy.
- Palantir (2022): ontology as systematic mapping of data to meaning, a layer separating pipelines from applications, with write-back and security embedded once; emphasis on feedback loops and digital representation of the business.
- Palantir marketing: three-part semantic, kinetic, dynamic framing (data, action, logic); docs rarely use 'dynamic' as a layer name.
- Independent practitioner (Caruso, Medium, 2025-06-13): defines kinetic as data mapping/ETL and dynamic as rules, permissions and lifecycle, which contradicts Palantir's own usage.
- Competitor (Elementum, 2026-07-16): the approach requires a separate indexed copy and vendor-led specialist mapping, producing lock-in and staffing dependency; Palantir's counterclaim is federation and virtual tables with no duplication.
- Trade press (Glitchwire, 2026-04-28): largely restates Palantir's April 2026 post; flags switching costs once an organization's operating logic is encoded in one vendor's platform.
- Partners (AWS APN blog 2021, PVM 2024, Cognizant 2025): corroborate decision write-back and the bootcamp format; Cognizant still describes it as 'a rich semantic layer', the label Palantir now rejects.
- Palantir's own stated limits: row and column controls are read-time only and do not propagate; scenarios are beta; bootcamps may yield no revenue (10-K risk factor).

Gaps:
- 'Connecting AI to Decisions with the Palantir Ontology' (Jan 2024) could not be read in full (HTTP 403, not in any reachable feed); exact date and author (believed Akshay Krishnaswamy, inferred from a reference in the Wilczynski post) unverified. Its argument is restated in the why-ontology docs and the 2026-04-28 blog, which were read in full.
- 'Deploying Full Spectrum AI in Days: How AIP Bootcamps Work' could not be read (HTTP 403). Bootcamp detail comes from the bootcamp product page, the FY2025 10-K, and a reseller (PVM) guide.
- No Palantir primary source was found that formally defines three named layers 'semantic, kinetic, dynamic'; only two marketing sentences use the triad. AIPCon talks and older Foundry PDFs were not reachable.
- Only 4 of the requested 8 web searches ran; the session's WebSearch budget (200 calls) was exhausted and further calls were refused. Compensated with roughly 45 documentation pages, 12 Palantir blog posts via RSS, the 10-K, and 6 independent pages.
- Trademark registration verified only for Palantir, Gotham, Palantir Foundry and the logo (per FY2025 10-K). Status of AIP, Apollo, Ontology, Workshop, Quiver and other names not checked against USPTO; no readable trademark list on palantir.com. The translation table therefore treats all capitalized product and feature names as proprietary.
- No independent analyst or academic treatment (Gartner, Forrester, peer-reviewed) was retrieved. Independent sources are one practitioner blog, one competitor blog, one AI-bylined trade-press item, and partner pieces.
- Palantir documentation pages are undated (footer copyright 2026); cited as accessed 2026-09-21. This matters for any article date constraint.
- Quotes from Cognizant were returned by the fetch tool's summarizer and not verified against raw HTML; AWS, Elementum, Glitchwire and PVM quotes were verified by grep against raw pages.
- 10-K text was extracted with a hand-written PDF decoder that drops apostrophes and some glyphs; only short clean spans are quoted and should be re-checked against the PDF before publication.
- Nothing found from Palantir specific to legal departments or legal operations; all worked examples are manufacturing, airline, hospital or defense.
- gtmfoundry.vc bootcamp go-to-market analysis failed to resolve (DNS).

Fact-checker overall: Notes file written to C:/Users/RALPHS~1/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--code-files-spaarke-website/aa6b682a-4844-485c-99e0-cb2228b928c3/scratchpad/research/palantir-intelligence-platform-model.verified.md (10-K text extract alongside it as pltr-10k-fy2025.txt). No repository files were touched.

Constraint: the session's WebSearch budget was already exhausted (200/200), so verification was by direct fetch only; where a cited page lacked the claimed text I walked sibling doc URLs. Palantir marketing pages are JS-rendered and Medium-hosted blog.palantir.com returns 403 to a plain fetch, so those were read through the r.jina.ai rendering proxy; the 10-K PDF was downloaded and text-extracted locally.

Result: 10 confirmed, 5 corrected, 0 refuted. The research is solid on substance; every primary exact_quote except one was seen verbatim. The one problem quote is finding 4: 'a developer toolchain that surfaces the full power of the semantic data, dynamic logic, and kinetic action integrated into the Ontology' is not on the AIP product page as published today, so the semantic=data / kinetic=action / dynamic=logic mapping should not be asserted as a Palantir primary statement until the quote is re-located. Other corrections are attribution-level: several details in finding 9 come from the use-case-life-cycle doc pages rather than the cited page; the 'never requires data to be duplicated' phrase is on the Multimodal Data Plane page, not the virtual tables page; the HR validation is 'such as', not 'only'; and the Elementum article's real title differs from the one given.

Writer cautions: (1) the staged-by-default / widen-autonomy sentences in why-ontology describe a fictional illustrative company (Onyx), not a documented product default; (2) 'only' four registered trademarks and 'AgentCamps is the newer name for bootcamps' are inferences, not statements in the sources; (3) unchecked sub-claims: Cognizant calling it a semantic layer, the Caruso Medium explainer, 'capturing decisions as data' in use-case docs, and process definitions with tasks/transitions/teams; (4) all Palantir docs pages are undated, so cite with an access date of 2026-09-21.


## research:consulting-voice-and-tone

I read 29 pages in full text (official HBR, strategy+business, and MIT SMR guidance; Barbara Minto's site; 11 HBR, 4 Deloitte Insights, 2 Bain, 3 MIT SMR, and 2 strategy+business articles, many on legal, AI, operations, and risk) and produced a notes file with 26 teachable traits, each with a verified quote of 30 words or fewer, URL, and date. Every quotation was machine-checked against downloaded source text. Core register: thesis within about 250 words, SCQA introductions, counted parallel frameworks, message-bearing headings, 15 to 25 word average sentences broken by short declaratives, 50 to 80 word paragraphs, inline-attributed statistics with sample and date, numbers interpreted skeptically, named and quantified one-paragraph cases, one calibrated hedge per claim, "we" and "you," parenthetical exhibit references, and endings on consequence rather than summary. Em dashes: all these publications use them (0 to 11 per 1,000 words, typically 2 to 7), but one September 2026 HBR article uses none and stays fully in register. The notes show four dash "jobs" and rewrite real sentences with colons, parentheses, semicolons, commas, or two sentences. Also covered: what the publications avoid, eight opening patterns, and tone versus marketing and academic prose. Major limit: mckinsey.com refused automated access, so there are no verbatim McKinsey quotes; McKinsey conventions are flagged as unverified prior knowledge. BCG was read only through a summarizer. The session search budget ran out after 9 searches. Helper scripts and downloaded pages sit in the scratchpad research/raw folder.

1. **HBR's official contributor guidelines define the target reader and explicitly reject unsurprising, promotional, and LLM-replicable ideas.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; 2026-06-10]
   Guidelines signed by Amy Bernstein and Maureen Hoch list five qualities: expertise, evidence, originality, usefulness, good writing. Readers are described as "smart, skeptical, and busy." The most common rejection reason is unsurprising findings. HBR refuses pieces that come across as promotional. The seven pitch questions work as a pre-draft checklist. For an AI drafting workflow the implication is that voice alone is insufficient; original evidence and point of view must be supplied.
   Quote: "The ideas should not be easily replicable by simply asking a large language model (LLM)."
   https://hbr.org/guidelines-for-authors
   FACT-CHECK: The page says 'One of the most common reasons we turn down proposals is because the findings or prescriptions aren't surprising' -- not 'the most common rejection reason'. Use 'one of the most common'. Fetched the page directly. Signed by Amy Bernstein (Editor in Chief, HBR) and Maureen Hoch (Editor, HBR.org); 'Last updated: June 10, 2026'. Five qualities (Expertise, Evidence, Originality, Usefulness, Good writing), 'Our readers are smart, skeptical, and busy', the LLM sentence (exact), the promotional exclusion, and seven pitch questions are all on the page. One wording overstatement in the finding's detail.
2. **strategy+business gives the most compact official definition of the register: clear, accessible, jargon-free, with intelligence, wit, and perspective; it declines promotional or narrowly functional pieces.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Writers' guidelines specify 500 to 3,500 words, a strategic (long-term, enterprise-level) frame, and readers who are thoughtful, pragmatic, and open to ideas beyond conventional wisdom. It declines articles that primarily market a product, consulting practice, or service, or that have a narrow functional focus. For legal operations this means tying tactics to enterprise outcomes. Page is undated (footer copyright 2026 PwC).
   Quote: "We seek clear, accessibly written articles that avoid jargon and approach readers with intelligence, wit, and perspective."
   https://www.strategy-business.com/press/writers
3. **MIT SMR's editorial director states the balance of rigor and utility, and explains endnotes as a credibility signal for practitioners; the MIT SMR author guidelines page is now a closure notice.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2019-09-10]
   Lisa Burrell's 2019 editor's letter names three criteria: ideas for a digital world, evidence-based thinking (lab experiments, field studies, data analysis, deep industry experience, synthesis), and accessible frameworks. The authors page (updated 2026-09-09, https://sloanreview.mit.edu/authors/) now states that print publication ceases after the fall issue, regular digital content continues through November 2026, and a new digital publication launches this winter, so detailed author guidance is no longer retrievable from the primary page.
   Quote: "Rigor is necessary but not sufficient. Our readers are busy, and they seek utility."
   https://sloanreview.mit.edu/article/what-we-publish-and-why/
4. **The Pyramid Principle's primary statement is a single sentence; SCQA, answer-first, MECE grouping, and action titles are the working rules taught around it, and published articles apply them in softened form.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Minto's own site names the introduction device the "Situation, Complication, Question Framework" for identifying the question in the reader's mind. Secondary consulting sources (Tom Spencer blog 2021-06-21; Management Consulted 2022-09-20; Slideworks 2023-09-20, ex-McKinsey and ex-BCG authors) add: answer first, group and summarize, order logically, MECE, and action titles under 15 words that state the takeaway so a reader of headings alone gets the argument. In the article corpus this appears as a thesis within about 250 words, message-bearing headings, and full-sentence exhibit captions (Bain 2019 caption: "Many AI uses by legal groups have already been proven").
   Quote: "The Minto Pyramid Principle says that your thinking will be easy for a reader to grasp if you present the ideas organized as a pyramid under a single point."
   https://www.barbaraminto.com/
   FACT-CHECK: Slideworks says action titles should be 'up to 15 words' (one or max two lines), not 'under 15 words'. Named Slideworks authors are Alexandra Hazard Kampmann and Mats Stigzelius ('Founder, ex-McKinsey consultant'); ex-BCG is claimed only for the site's template makers generally, not confirmed for these authors. Minto quote exact on barbaraminto.com; the site names the 'Situation, Complication, Question Framework (SCQ Framework)' for 'identifying the question in your reader's mind' (SCQ, not SCQA, on the primary site). Tom Spencer post confirmed: 'Guide for Effective Writing' by Jason Oh, 2021-06-21, now served at spencertom.com (tomspencer.com.au refuses connections). Management Consulted SCQA page datePublished 2022-09-20 confirmed (body not read in detail). Slideworks action-titles page 'Updated: Sep 20, 2023' confirmed. Bain Figure 2 caption exact. 'Thesis within ~250 words' is researcher analysis.
5. **Em dashes are universal in these publications but not essential to the register: density ranged from 0 to 11 per 1,000 words across 16 articles, and a September 2026 HBR article of 1,756 words contains none.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; primary; 2026-09-21]
   Researcher's automated counts: MIT SMR column 11.0 per 1,000 words; HBR pieces 2.6 to 8.4; Deloitte 1.7 to 5.8; Bain 2019 legal article 1.4; s+b 6.2; HBR 'True Cost of Corporate Scandals' 0.0. Official guideline pages of HBR, MIT SMR, and s+b also use them. HBR magazine, Deloitte, Bain, and s+b set the dash closed; MIT SMR and older HBR digital posts set it spaced (AP style). The zero-dash HBR article relies on colons, commas, and short sentences, for example: "People are watching. And when they do not like what they see, they walk."
   Quote: "People are watching. And when they do not like what they see, they walk."
   https://hbr.org/2026/09/research-the-true-cost-of-corporate-scandals-talent
6. **The em dash does four jobs in this genre (paired aside, inserted list, end-of-sentence punch, trailing elaboration), and each has a clean substitute: parentheses, colon, period or semicolon, or comma plus reduced clause.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-14]
   The notes rewrite nine real sentences. Example: the quoted HBR sentence becomes "If they cannot complete it, they do not have a transformation project. They have a technology experiment." Another: "Their prescription for incumbents is exactly right: rearchitect before you automate." Two-sentence rewrites are usually stronger because they create the short declarative the genre favors. Warning recorded: do not swap every dash for a comma, which produces run-ons; identify the job first. The colon is the natural workhorse (20 colons in 2,376 words in the same HBR article) and semicolons remain in active use in HBR and Bain.
   Quote: "If they cannot complete it, they do not have a transformation project—only a technology experiment."
   https://hbr.org/2026/09/stop-automating-old-processes-design-new-ones-instead
7. **Statistics are attributed inline with source, instrument, sample, and date in the same sentence, and are usually given a comparison (base, peer, or before and after).** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-14]
   No orphan percentages were found in the corpus. HBR, Deloitte, Bain, BCG, and s+b use numerals with the % sign; MIT SMR and Deloitte spell out a number that starts a sentence. Bain's 2019 legal article pairs legal's 20% to 25% AI adoption with 40% in finance and 54% in HR. Method is disclosed in one or two plain sentences early (HBR 2026-09-21: "Our final sample covers 735 of the largest U.S. public companies over eight years").
   Quote: "BCG’s January 2026 “AI Radar,” surveying 640 CEOs across 16 markets, reports that corporations expect to roughly double AI spending this year, from 0.8% of revenue to about 1.7%."
   https://hbr.org/2026/09/stop-automating-old-processes-design-new-ones-instead
8. **The register earns trust by telling the reader what a number does and does not prove, and by conceding the best objection once.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-14]
   After citing Wayfair's 2.5 million corrected tags and 41,000 automated tickets per month, the HBR authors immediately limit the claim. Bain's 2019 legal article uses the classic one-sentence concession: "To be sure, AI still gets a lot of hype from vendors and the technology media, but plenty of cases have delivered substantial business value". This measured move is the clearest separator from marketing copy.
   Quote: "Those figures demonstrate the scale of the redesigned workflow, not by themselves its business value"
   https://hbr.org/2026/09/stop-automating-old-processes-design-new-ones-instead
9. **Examples are named, quantified, one paragraph long, and built on three beats (situation, action, measured result); when a client cannot be named, the anonymization is specific about sector, region, and scale.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2019-02-05]
   Bain's legal department article gives JPMorgan (360,000 hours of review cut), Coca-Cola, and Liberty Mutual cases, each in a single paragraph introduced with 'for instance' or 'Consider'. Deloitte's anonymized form: "a leading European telecommunications provider consolidated four separate knowledge silos serving 19 million customers". HBR pairs a failure case with a success case and states exactly what differs (Deloitte Australia versus Wayfair).
   Quote: "Coca-Cola launched a self-service portal that reduced contract-drafting time for many matters from up to 10 hours to around 15 minutes."
   https://www.bain.com/insights/corporate-legal-eagles-start-to-embrace-artificial-intelligence/
10. **Headings carry a message or instruction rather than a topic, with three house variants plus a new AI-search variant.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; 2026-08-20]
   HBR: title case tied to the framework ("Decision 2: Decide Where Human Authority Remains"). Deloitte: sentence case message phrases. Bain 2019: sentence-case gerund phrases ("Turning digital hype into practical reality"), with bold run-in imperatives inside sections ("Think big."). Bain 2026 (https://www.bain.com/insights/how-to-architect-for-agentic-ai/, 2026-05-29) uses question headings answered by a first sentence that restates the question, which reads as formatted for AI retrieval and is more repetitive for human readers. No article uses headings called Introduction or Conclusion.
   Quote: "Start with capabilities, not channels"
   https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/insights/topics/technology-management/enterprise-ai-control-plane.html
   FACT-CHECK: Deloitte prints the publish date as 21 Aug. 2026; the researcher's 2026-08-20 is the JSON-LD UTC timestamp (2026-08-20T18:30Z). Cite 21 August 2026.
