Deloitte heading 'Start with capabilities, not channels' and article title confirmed. HBR heading 'Decision 2: Decide Where Human Authority Remains' confirmed. Bain 2019 headings ('Still lagging other functions', 'Turning digital hype into practical reality') and bold run-ins ('Think big.' etc.) confirmed. Bain 2026 (datePublished 2026-05-29) confirmed: question H2s each answered by a first sentence restating the question. 'Formatted for AI retrieval' is the researcher's inference. 'No Introduction/Conclusion headings' true of all pages I opened, not checked across all 16.
11. **There are two hedging dialects: HBR authors commit and limit scope precisely, while firm-published research (Deloitte especially) hedges densely with could, may, and appears to.** [CORRECTED; confidence high; primary; 2025-12-17]
   HBR example: "Our research suggests that assumption is wrong." (2026-09-21). Deloitte's honest-scope model is worth copying: "The scenarios that follow are plausible depictions of future states, not forecasts." (2026-09-09). But Deloitte's "organizations could be up to 88 times more likely" is both hedged and extravagant. Recommendation in the notes: one hedge per claim, placed on the verb, never stacked; prefer the HBR dialect for Spaarke and borrow from firms only exhibit discipline, method disclosure, and message headings.
   Quote: "Taken together, these patterns suggest that AI-driven business outcomes peak when decision rights are distributed among complementary leaders."
   https://www.deloitte.com/us/en/insights/topics/digital-transformation/c-suite-leadership-ai-returns.html
   FACT-CHECK: Deloitte's printed dates are one day later than the researcher's (which are JSON-LD UTC timestamps at T18:30Z): C-suite article is 18 Dec. 2025, not 17 Dec.; agents article is 10 Sep. 2026, not 9 Sep. All quotes exact: 'Taken together, these patterns suggest...' and 'organizations could be up to 88 times more likely to achieve high ROI...' (Deloitte C-suite article); 'Our research suggests that assumption is wrong.' (HBR 2026-09-21); 'The scenarios that follow are plausible depictions of future states, not forecasts.' (Deloitte 'AI agents in the human workplace'). The two-dialect generalization and the recommendation are researcher judgment from a small sample.
12. **Articles end on consequence, not summary: a short final section that returns to the opening frame, often a contrasted pair of futures and a last sentence under 20 words.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-14]
   HBR marks the turn with a typographic break instead of a heading; Deloitte uses a forward-looking heading. Other closers: MIT SMR 2026-03-30 "Let the machine do the lifting, not the leading."; Deloitte 2026-09-09 "In a future crowded with agents, speed to market will matter, but not as much as trust, governance, and human capabilities." Sections likewise close on a compact takeaway ("It is to be deliberate everywhere."). Many pieces also hand the reader a tool before leaving: diagnostic questions, a checklist, or a fill-in sentence.
   Quote: "Companies that do that will create better work. Those that do not will simply produce faster messes."
   https://hbr.org/2026/09/stop-automating-old-processes-design-new-ones-instead
13. **Eight opening patterns recur; the most reusable for a general counsel audience is 'received view, then reversal' backed by the author's own evidence.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2026-09-21]
   Patterns documented with quotes: data tension (HBR 2026-09-14); received view then reversal (HBR 2026-09-21, reversal line "Our research suggests that assumption is wrong."); named scene (HBR 2026-09-17); second-person provocation (HBR 2004-12, "Your company’s legal department is broken."); big number plus boardroom question (Deloitte 2026-06-17); classic SCQA (HBR 2012-07, turn sentence "That close relationship is being disrupted."); neglected relationship (HBR 2016-07-25); plain question (Deloitte 2025-12-17). All deliver the thesis within roughly 250 words.
   Quote: "The traditional way companies think about regulatory penalties is almost entirely financial."
   https://hbr.org/2026/09/research-the-true-cost-of-corporate-scandals-talent
14. **Measured rhythm: average sentence length of about 15 to 25 words in 14 of 16 articles, 10 to 25 percent of HBR sentences at eight words or fewer, and paragraphs averaging 50 to 80 words; practice is less austere than writing-coach advice.** [CONFIRMED; confidence medium; primary; 2026-09-14]
   Researcher's automated counts (regex sentence splitting, approximate). Exceptions: Heineman's 2016 HBR essay averages about 30 words per sentence and is the stiffest prose in the sample; the MIT SMR panel report is inflated by long quotations. Paragraph means: Deloitte 50 to 61, Bain 2019 77, s+b 69, MIT SMR column 54. HBR paragraph lengths could not be measured. Coaches quoted in HBR (Garner, Bernoff) urge short sentences, yet every article carried sentences of 30 or more words; the real discipline is variation and one idea per sentence.
   Quote: "The goal of the 4A framework is not to be conservative everywhere. It is to be deliberate everywhere."
   https://hbr.org/2026/09/stop-automating-old-processes-design-new-ones-instead
15. **Legal-trained writers must invert their habitual structure, and practitioners explicitly treat McKinsey Quarterly as a style model.** [CONFIRMED; confidence high; primary; 2014-11-20]
   HBR's 2014 business-writing guide profiles a Harvard Law graduate who joined McKinsey and was told to get to the point faster: legal and academic writing builds to the conclusion, business writing starts with it. A second profiled consultant "began reading business publications, like McKinsey Quarterly, for style." Bryan Garner, quoted in the same piece: "One of the great diseases of business writing is postponing the message to the middle part of the writing". He also advises contractions, verbs over -ion nouns, and a buzzword blacklist (actionable, core competency, impactful, incentivize).
   Quote: "But in business communications, it’s best to start with your conclusion first."
   https://hbr.org/2014/11/how-to-improve-your-business-writing

Viewpoints:
- Answer first (Minto, Garner, consulting training) versus narrative hook (HBR features often spend two or three paragraphs on a scene or tension first). Both reach the thesis within about 250 words; a recorded critique of answer-first is that readers who reject the headline claim may skip the support.
- Austere coaching advice (short sentences, simple words) versus actual published practice (about 20 words per sentence on average, with regular 30-plus word sentences). The working discipline is variation and one idea per sentence, not brevity as such.
- Commitment versus caution: HBR academic and practitioner authors state findings flatly and scope them precisely; consulting-firm research, Deloitte above all, wraps similar claims in could, may, and appears to, which reads as legal caution.
- Em dashes are universal in these outlets but dispensable: density varies from 0 to 11 per 1,000 words inside the same publication family, and a zero-dash 2026 HBR article reads fully in register.
- Headings for human readers (message phrases, imperatives) versus headings for machine retrieval (Bain 2026 question headings with answer-first sentences that restate the question). The latter is efficient for AI search and more repetitive to read.
- The corrective 'not X, but Y' frame is a hallmark of HBR argument and also a widely recognized marker of machine-written prose in 2026. The publications earn it by attributing X to a real, named belief; the researcher recommends a cap for the drafting agent (own recommendation, not sourced).
- HBR's 2026 guidelines hold that ideas an LLM could produce unaided are not publishable, which implies voice imitation alone will not place an article in this genre; original data, cases, and point of view must be supplied by Spaarke.
- Two dialects inside the register: warm, direct, second-person HBR and MIT SMR author pieces versus cooler, third-person, bulleted, survey-heavy firm research. The researcher recommends the HBR dialect for a small company addressing expert practitioners.

Gaps:
- No McKinsey primary text: mckinsey.com timed out on four fetch-tool requests, one direct download never connected, and one public reader-proxy attempt returned an explicit 403 Access Denied, after which I stopped. No McKinsey Insights or McKinsey Quarterly article and no McKinsey Publishing editorial statement was read. The brief's request for McKinsey articles is unmet; the corpus is 11 HBR, 4 Deloitte, 2 Bain, 3 MIT SMR, 2 strategy+business. McKinsey conventions mentioned in the notes (spelled-out 'percent', '(Exhibit 1)' references) are from prior knowledge and need a human spot check in a browser.
- BCG: one article read only through the fetch tool's summarizer (bcg.com returned 403 to direct download). BCG quotations, including the '(See Exhibit 1.)' form, are unverified; the summarizer's offered em-dash example contained no em dash, so its verbatim reliability is doubtful.
- MIT SMR detailed author guidelines (length, article types, tone rules) are no longer on the primary page, which now carries a closure notice dated 2026-09-09; the support-site copy redirects to a login and was not pursued.
- No interview or essay by a McKinsey or HBR editor analyzing house prose style was found before the shared session search budget (200 calls) ran out; only 9 of my searches executed. The editor and coach strand rests on three HBR business-writing articles (Birchard 2021, Bernoff 2016, O'Hara 2014) and ex-consultant secondary sources.
- Barbara Minto's book was not consulted; her site provides only the one-sentence principle, and SCQA and MECE details come from secondary sources (Tom Spencer blog, Management Consulted, Slideworks, ModelThinkers via summarizer).
- HBR paragraph lengths could not be measured because article text was available only as one unbroken block in page structured data; sentence statistics across the corpus are approximate (regex splitting, headings and table text mixed into HBR blocks).
- Undated or partially dated sources: strategy+business writers' guidelines (copyright 2026 only), barbaraminto.com, ModelThinkers, and the Avery Content post (shows 'Sep 27' without a year).
- No public style guide was found for Deloitte Insights, Bain, or BCG.
- Process disclosure: besides the notes file, I wrote helper scripts and downloaded page copies to the session scratchpad at research/raw (not in any git repository) so that all 100-plus quotations could be machine-verified against source text. No repository file was modified.

Fact-checker overall: Every direct quote in this track was found verbatim on the cited page, and the headline numbers (1,756 words with zero em dashes; 2,376 words with 20 colons; Bain paragraph mean 77; Bain 1.4 dashes per 1,000 words) reproduced exactly when I recounted. HBR articles are paywalled to WebFetch, so I read the full articleBody from each page's JSON-LD via curl and string-matched the quotes; raw pages are cached in the scratchpad fc/ folder. WebSearch budget was exhausted for the session, so all checks were direct URL fetches, including URLs taken from the researcher's notes file for items the findings cited without a link. Four detail corrections: (1) Deloitte Insights dates are consistently one day early because the researcher used JSON-LD UTC timestamps; printed dates are 21 Aug. 2026, 18 Dec. 2025, 10 Sep. 2026 and 18 Jun. 2026. (2) HBR guidelines say 'one of the most common reasons', not 'the most common reason'. (3) Slideworks says action titles 'up to 15 words', not 'under 15', and ex-BCG authorship is not confirmed for the named authors. (4) s+b never uses the word 'enterprise'; that is a gloss. Also note the Deloitte European-telecom example sits in the knowledge-management article, not in any URL attached to that finding, and the Tom Spencer post now lives at spencertom.com. Interpretive claims (four jobs of the dash, two hedging dialects, 'most reusable' opening, 'no orphan percentages', corpus-wide generalizations over 16 articles) are the researcher's analysis; I confirmed the evidence they rest on, not the generalizations. Verification notes written to C:/Users/RALPHS~1/AppData/Local/Temp/claude/c--code-files-spaarke-website/aa6b682a-4844-485c-99e0-cb2228b928c3/scratchpad/research/consulting-voice-and-tone.verified.md. No repository files were modified.


## gap:intake-and-self-service-named-cases

WebSearch budget was already exhausted (200/200) when this task began, so zero searches ran; all evidence comes from about 60 direct WebFetch calls. gartner.com, acc.com, legalops.com and web.archive.org were blocked, and CLOC agenda pages render client-side. ACC Value Champions, CLOC/LegalOps.com awards, CCBJ and WorldCC benchmarks were therefore NOT reached and remain gaps.

Gartner: two independent reprints (SMBtech 2026-05-27, carrying release boilerplate; Lawyers Weekly 2026-06-09) match word for word. It is a direct quote from analyst Weston Wicks, release dated 2026-05-26, horizon 2029, population simply "legal departments" with no survey or sample. Correct wording is "without human intervention", and "one-half" refers to requests captured by adopting departments.

Best non-vendor named cases come from ACC Corporate Counsel Now (2026-09-16): Liberty Mutual reviews about 2,500 NDAs a year, lawyer handling cut from over an hour to 15-20 minutes, self-service tool still in pilot; Palo Alto Networks cut routine turnaround from three-to-five days to one, saving 20-40 hours monthly, and is the only case listing intake fields. ACC/Everlaw 2026: only 28 percent of teams track time to resolution.

Vendor-published named cases (Checkbox, Streamline AI, Josef, Juro) give NDA-specific deflection (Coca-Cola Europacific Partners 90 percent; Align 5-7 percent needing redlines) and cycle time (Elastic 23 to 3 days). No case publishes a whole-front-door deflection rate. Tool results also carried a repeated injected reminder block, which I ignored and flagged.

Viewpoints:
- Analyst forecast view (Gartner): AI-driven intake answering half of requests without human intervention becomes majority practice by 2029; this is a prediction with no stated survey base, not evidence of current practice.
- Practitioner view via ACC: current real programs are narrow and NDA- or template-specific, often built in-house (Liberty Mutual's Legal Data Science team, Palo Alto Networks on Gemini), with modest, honest metrics and pilots still running.
- Vendor view: legal front door case studies emphasize adoption, speed of deployment and NDA volumes, and rarely disclose intake schema, total request volume or a whole-department deflection rate.
- Measurement gap view (ACC / Everlaw 2026): most departments still do not track time to resolution, so the data that intake systems generate is largely unused for service reporting.
- Process view (Elastic): most NDA delay sat upstream of legal review, in deciding whose paper to use, which suggests intake design, not lawyer speed, is the main lever.

Gaps:
- WebSearch budget was exhausted (200 of 200) before this task started: zero searches could be run, so the instruction to run at least 8 distinct searches could not be met. All work was done by direct WebFetch (about 60 fetches). Search-engine pages fetched via WebFetch were unusable (CAPTCHA, 429, 403, junk results).
- ACC Value Champions 2024, 2025 and 2026 winner write-ups not reviewed: acc.com returned 403 on every route, including via a reader proxy; web.archive.org is not fetchable by the tool.
- CLOC and LegalOps.com award winners not reviewed: legalops.com returned 403; cloc.org home page exposes no awards link.
- CLOC Global Institute 2026 (Chicago, 11-14 May 2026) session descriptions not retrieved: agenda and daily pages render sessions client-side, returning navigation only. ACC Legal Operations session descriptions not reached.
- CCBJ case articles not reached (site search returned an empty body). ACC Docket now redirects to corporatecounselnow.com, whose on-site search returned no results for 'intake' or 'value champions'.
- World Commerce and Contracting benchmark on NDA cycle time and volume not found; no neutral NDA cycle-time benchmark located.
- Gartner primary press release (2026-05-26) and the Gartner intake and triage case study both returned 403. Whether the original carries any methodology or population footnote is unverified; US-spelling original wording not seen.
- No vendor metric is independently audited. Checkbox page dates (July-August 2026) appear to be site rebuild dates, so the true dates of those programs are unknown; Streamline AI and Josef pages are undated.
- Sample size of the ACC / Everlaw 2026 survey not confirmed. Juro case metrics taken from index pages only. Ironclad, LawVu and Tonkean yielded no usable intake or self-service metric.
- Liberty Mutual's self-service NDA tool is a pilot with no outcome data yet.


## gap:association-definitions-of-legal-ops

All four bodies were checked against primary text. WebSearch was exhausted and WebFetch refused verbatim output, so pages were fetched with curl and parsed locally. ACC's Maturity Model 2.0 was re-read from its PDF.

- **CLOC:** No one-sentence definition; describes legal ops professionals as "the chief operating officers of the legal team". Core 12 captured verbatim; last revised 7 April 2020, web copy modified May 2025.
- **ACC:** Definition and 14 functions captured verbatim. PDF created 29 September 2020, re-saved October 2024; the original model was 2017.
- **LegalOps.com:** Live site read directly; definition and mission match staging, but the live site has diverged. The 15 practice areas were read from an on-page image; per-area definitions are member-gated.
- **Legal Operators:** Publishes no definition or framework, only a content-tag list.

**Four topics:**
- Intake: not a function anywhere; only ACC names it, as a marker inside two functions. Self-service: absent from all.
- BI is a named function in each framework.
- KM is a named function everywhere and defined without AI by CLOC and ACC.
- AI appears once in CLOC (Technology) and four times in ACC, never in KM, and in no LegalOps.com area name. AI is entering through CLOC's September 2026 certification instead.

The organization behind RLLB is LegalOps.com (Brenton and Franke), not Legal Operators (McCarthy).

Viewpoints:
- CLOC defines legal ops by role and stature: 'chief operating officers of the legal team' and 'strategic business partner'. Its framework is a list of 12 functional areas. In the September 2026 certification release CLOC says it 'built this profession' and 'defined its foundational framework, the CLOC Core 12'.
- ACC defines legal ops as a business-management discipline ('rooted in business fundamentals, leveraging processes, data and technology'). Its framework is a crowd-sourced, three-stage maturity model across 14 functions. It is the only one of the three that names intake and triage, and the only one with standalone Contract Management, eDiscovery, IP Management, Innovation Management and Change Management functions.
- LegalOps.com (Brenton and Franke) defines legal operations management by outcome thresholds ('quality, cost, and risk thresholds for the enterprise') and frames the end goal as competitive advantage. It claims lineage over the CLOC framework: Franke's bio credits him with 'creating CLOC's 12 Core Competencies'. It positions its reference models against communities that 'focus on basic networking'.
- Legal Operators takes no definitional position. It presents itself as a peer community ('content, connections, and tech') and organises content by technology category rather than by competency.
- Common blind spot: all three published frameworks were written before generative AI (2020, 2020 and October 2023). None treats intake / the legal front door or self-service as a function, and both CLOC and ACC define KM without AI. AI is now arriving through adjacent programmes (CLOC's LOCP credential and AI Intensive; RLLB's 2026 AI track) rather than through revised framework text.
- Lineage tension: CLOC's 2026 releases say it was 'Founded in 2015' and that the Core 12 has defined excellence 'since 2016'. LegalOps.com says Brenton and Franke 'founded ... CLOC in 2016' and that Franke created the original 12 competencies. An article citing both should attribute carefully and avoid taking sides on who 'built' the profession.

Gaps:
- ACC: no ACC-authored statement of the Maturity Model 2.0 launch date was reachable. The year 2020 rests on PDF metadata (created 2020-09-29) plus the PDF's 'original model in 2017' sentence. acc.com HTML returned 403 to curl, WebFetch and the Jina reader, and the Internet Archive was offline.
- ACC: I could not determine whether the October 2024 re-save of the PDF changed content. I could not check whether ACC has issued anything newer than 2.0 or an AI addendum.
- CLOC: I could not determine whether Core 12 web wording changed at the 2024 site relaunch or the May 2025 edits, because the Internet Archive was offline.
- CLOC: the member-only Core 12 Maturity Assessment Playbook and the Compass tool were not read. How their stage descriptors treat intake, self-service, BI, KM or AI is unknown.
- CLOC: the forthcoming 'CLOC Industry Standards' (announced for the 2027 Global Institute) are not public.
- CLOC: the '67% developing' KM maturity figure on the KM page is not tied to a named State of the Industry edition.
- LegalOps.com: per-practice-area definitions for the 15-area model are member-gated. Its treatment of intake, self-service, BI and KM is inferred from area names only. No revision date is published for the reference models. The October 2023 unveiling comes from LawVision, an event partner.
- Legal Operators: there is no framework to analyse. Colin McCarthy's founder status rests on a 2022 podcast page, not on the organization's site. His current role is unconfirmed, and no 2026 content was found on the public site.
- Search: the WebSearch budget was exhausted (200/200) and most public search engines blocked automated queries. Discovery relied on direct primary URLs, cloc.org site search, one successful Brave query and geeklawblog site search. A press release or trade article dating ACC 2.0 more precisely may exist but was not found.


## gap:conference-session-sweep-2026

WebSearch was unavailable (session budget 200/200 already spent; all three attempts refused), so I ran zero general searches and relied on ~45 direct fetches of primary pages and on-site search pages of the named outlets. Load-bearing sources were read as raw page/PDF text, so quotes are verbatim. Filled: (1) CLOC CGI 2026 full official agenda (117 items) with titles, speakers, orgs for data/BI, KM and intake sessions; post-event release (nearly 2,400 attendees; Snell quote). (2) Harbor 2026 Legal Department Maturity Index press release verified: 98% implementing/exploring AI, 57% live, 24% pilot, 81% rank technology top priority; sample size NOT disclosed. (3) CLOC Compass launched 2026-05-12 (Core 12, four stages, members-only beta); no findings published. (4) ILTACON 2026: 5,780 attendees; session catalog not public; Artificial Lawyer/Draftwise daily briefings, Harbor's 2026-09-11 brief (KM sessions: clean connected data precondition; AI governance ownership fragmented across IT, Risk, GC, KM), three CCBJ pieces, LawSites round-ups (Entegrata semantic layer, NetDocuments context graph). (5) ILTA 2026 Technology Survey public executive summary (508 firms; 94% engaged with GenAI; knowledge infrastructure "dramatically underdeveloped"; firms anticipate KM and data-science headcount). (6) RLLB: one new trade-press recap, CCBJ 2026-09-18, with iFood intake example. Not retrieved: ACC 2026 Annual Meeting (acc.com 403), RLLB keynotes/awards, LinkedIn recaps, Legal IT Insider.

1. **Harbor's 2026 Legal Department Maturity Index Survey (released at CLOC CGI, 11 May 2026) reports 98% of legal departments implementing or exploring AI, 57% live and 24% in pilot; sample size is not disclosed.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; vendor; 2026-05-11]

Viewpoints:
- Association view (CLOC, Oyango Snell): the AI conversation has moved from what AI might do to what it has done, what broke, and how to govern it; legal ops is building, proving and leading.
- Consultancy view (Harbor: Kevin Clem, Justin Farmer): adoption is near-universal but the operating model lags; measure outcomes not logins; govern data one recurring deliverable at a time; AI governance needs a single clear owner because ownership is fragmented across IT, Risk, GC and KM.
- DMS-vendor view (NetDocuments' Josh Baxter and Heath Harris; iManage's Paul Walker and Ivy Grey): models are rented and interfaces swap out, institutional context is permanent; AI has sent the industry 'right back at taxonomy'; librarians and KM staff hold the needed expertise; bring the application to the knowledge.
- Data-platform vendor view (Entegrata's Tom Baldwin): the debate over a dedicated data layer is over; firms now compete on speed to ship one, with semantic layers and MCP servers exposing governed data to any AI tool.
- Law-firm practitioner view (Tim Fox, Ogletree Deakins): shift value talk from efficiency to enablement; firm-wide time-savings totals are noise unless connected accurately in the data; every AI workflow keeps a human in the loop.
- In-house practitioner view (iFood and Salesforce at RLLB; Westpac and JPMorganChase at CGI): embed risk thresholds into intake flows so low-risk work proceeds without lawyers; use invoice data to allocate outside counsel work; build executive dashboards that answer what legal costs and what it delivers; treat KM as AI-enabled knowledge discovery.
- Trade-press analyst and sceptic view (Kristin Calve, CCBJ; Bob Ambrogi, LawSites): the real question is cost-justified 'good enough'; value settles deeper into the stack with institutional knowledge and entitlements; vendor benchmarks should be read as vendor benchmarks; 'peak legal tech' spending signals capital misallocation.
- Survey-author view (ILTA 2026 Technology Survey): platform migration is largely complete but the governance migration has yet to begin; knowledge infrastructure is dramatically underdeveloped; success depends on process discipline, data strategy and systems integration rather than GenAI itself.

Gaps:
- WebSearch budget for the session was already exhausted (200/200); zero general web searches could be run, so the 'at least 8 searches' requirement was not met. Coverage relies on direct fetches and on-site search pages of named outlets; anything only discoverable via a general search engine may have been missed.
- ACC 2026 Annual Meeting: acc.com returned HTTP 403 on every route (fetch tool and raw HTTP) and the Wayback Machine is blocked to the tool. Dates, city, agenda status and sessions are unconfirmed.
- Harbor 2026 Legal Department Maturity Index: sample size, methodology and field dates are not disclosed in the press release, and no public report page exists. Whether it is a new survey or a re-cut of the 135-department 2025 Harbor/CLOC Law Department Survey is not stated.
- CLOC Compass aggregate findings: none published as of 2026-09-21 (tool is a members-only beta). The CLOC Talk podcast episode on Compass was not reviewed.
- ILTACON 2026 official session catalog is not public (iltacon.org links a stale 2023 spreadsheet; event app and recordings are member or purchase only). KM and data session titles, other than those named in trade coverage, could not be listed. The specific KM-track session titles and speakers remain unknown.
- ILTA KM community: no 2026 KM white paper, KM survey, or statement on KM team roles in AI programs was found; the executive summary's only KM-role statement is that firms anticipate added KM headcount, with no percentage. Full survey results are members-only.
- RLLB 2026: keynote or plenary topics still unidentified; 2026 award winners not posted (awards page still shows 2025); no confirmed session title on KM or intake; no recap on Artificial Lawyer, LawSites, Above the Law, 3 Geeks, Legal.io, JD Supra, The Edge Room, Brightflag or Checkbox. Only new item is the CCBJ piece of 2026-09-18.
- LinkedIn speaker recaps for all four events could not be reached (LinkedIn is not fetchable).
- Legal IT Insider (legaltechnology.com) is behind a bot wall; its ILTACON 2026 coverage was not read. Harbor's Legal Soundings ILTACON podcast is audio only and was not reviewed.
- No statistic specific to intake or self-service (volumes, deflection rates) surfaced from any of the four conferences.
- Items read only through the summarizing fetch model (ILTA wrap-up page, ILTACON keynotes page, CLOC Compass pages, LawSites round-up part 4, two ILTA blog posts) should have wording re-verified before quotation.


## gap:bi-technology-state-and-deterministic-role

IMPORTANT METHOD LIMIT: WebSearch was refused (session budget of 200 already spent), and public search engines fetched via WebFetch were blocked. I ran zero keyword searches. Instead I retrieved about 75 primary pages directly (vendor docs, newsrooms, release notes, analyst blogs, plus on-site searches/indexes) and copied every quote from the downloaded page text (curly quotes normalised). gartner.com, tableau.com marketing pages and powerbi.microsoft.com were HTTP 403, so no Gartner text was read; 2026 Magic Quadrant content is vendor characterisation only.

Findings: (a) As of Sept 2026 the conversational layer is GA at Tableau (Next GA 2025-04-15; Tableau Agent conversational GA 2026-05-05), Databricks (Genie GA 2025-06-12; Genie Spaces renamed Genie Agents 2026-07-08), Snowflake (Intelligence and Cortex Agents GA 2025-11-04; Cortex Analyst being folded into Cortex Agents 2026-08-28), Looker (GA "last year") and Fabric data agents (GA 2026-03-18). Power BI's report Copilot pane is GA but the standalone Copilot is still preview. (b) OSI launched 2025-09-23, entered Apache incubation 2026-06-22 as Apache Ossie, 50+ organisations, spec still a 0.2.0 draft. dbt's April 2026 benchmark: semantic layer 98 to 100% vs text-to-SQL 84 to 90%. (c) "Deterministic" is vendor language (dbt, Looker, ThoughtSpot, Tableau Pulse); Forrester says shared semantic layer as enterprise infrastructure; BARC is a counterweight. Power BI Copilot can author new DAX, so "call, never recompute" is not universal. (d) Microsoft: 30M+ monthly users (Sept 2025), 35M (Mar 2026). (e) Alert-to-workflow patterns documented for Power BI, Activator, Tableau, Looker, ThoughtSpot.

1. **Power BI Copilot release status is mixed as of late August 2026: the in-report Copilot pane is GA, the standalone cross-item Copilot and Copilot in apps remain preview, and Copilot requires Fabric F2+ or Premium P1+ capacity (Pro or PPU licence alone is insufficient).** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; primary; 2026-08-24]
   Microsoft Learn 'Overview of Copilot for Power BI' (ms.date 2026-08-24, updated 2026-08-31). Second quote on same page: 'The Power BI agent available as a standalone, full-screen experience accessible from the Power BI left navigation is in preview.' Standalone page (https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/power-bi/create-reports/copilot-chat-with-data-standalone, last updated 2026-08-27) is still titled '(preview)'. 'Prep data for AI' (verified answers, AI instructions) also still preview.
   Quote: "The report agent Copilot pane available on the right side of reports is generally available."
   https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/power-bi/create-reports/copilot-introduction
2. **Power BI Copilot prefers governed semantic-model measures but can also generate new DAX, so it is not a strict 'call the governed metric, never recompute' design.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; primary; 2026-07-06]
   Important nuance for the deterministic thesis. From the standalone Copilot doc (ms.date 2026-07-06, last updated 2026-08-27). Same doc warns that unprepared models give 'limited or inaccurate' answers and that models not approved for Copilot show a low-quality warning.
   Quote: "it then uses the measures and other data fields in your semantic model (or even creates new DAX calculations) to generate the answer for you."
   https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/power-bi/create-reports/copilot-chat-with-data-standalone
3. **Fabric data agents are generally available (announced 2026-03-18), are strictly read-only, can use Power BI semantic models and ontologies as sources, and can be invoked from Microsoft 365 Copilot, Copilot Studio, Foundry and Teams.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; primary; 2026-05-11]
   GA announcement by Arun Ulag on Azure Blog 2026-03-18: 'I'm excited to share that Fabric data agents are now generally available.' Microsoft Learn concept page (ms.date 2026-05-11, updated 2026-09-09) confirms GA and says: 'The Fabric data agent strictly enforces read-only access, maintaining read-only data connections to all data sources.' Up to five sources per agent; NL2SQL, NL2DAX, NL2KQL. Doc suggests routing 'questions about financial metrics to a Power BI semantic model'.
   Quote: "Data agent in Microsoft Fabric is a generally available feature that enables you to build your own conversational Q&A systems by using generative AI."
   https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/fabric/data-science/concept-data-agent

   Salesforce newsroom announcement. Same page: 'Tableau Semantics serves as the semantic layer, providing Tableau Next and Agentforce with a unified understanding of business data.' Concierge and Data Pro skills were slated for June 2025, Inspector 'in 2025'.

   Status quote from Google Cloud blog 2026-07-28. Architecture from Looker docs (https://cloud.google.com/looker/docs/conversational-analytics-overview, last updated 2026-09-18): 'Instead of querying your database directly, Conversational Analytics determines which fields, filters, sorts, and limits should be used in the query.' followed by 'Looker then composes and executes the query by using the underlying LookML model.' Dashboard Agents, Agentic Workflows and managed MCP server are preview (Next '26 post, 2026-04-22).

   https://cloud.google.com/blog/products/data-analytics/conversational-analytics-in-google-data-cloud-in-q326

   Jason Ganz and Benoit Perigaud, 2026-04-07. ACME Insurance benchmark (Sequeda et al.), 11 questions x 20 runs, open source (dbt-labs/dbt-llm-sl-bench). On the original normalized schema, in-scope questions: SL 100% vs text-to-SQL 62.5%/51.2%. Full-set text-to-SQL rose from 32.7% (2023) to 64.5% (2026), so the gap is narrowing. Caveats: vendor-run, small benchmark, text-to-SQL given the whole schema. Other quotes: 'With text-to-SQL, failure looks like a plausible but incorrect answer. With the Semantic Layer, failure looks like an error message.' and 'When accuracy matters (board data, auditors, OKRs, KPIs, weekly reports): configure a Semantic Layer and connect your LLM to it.'

Microsoft equivalent: Power BI MCP servers doc (ms.date 2026-09-01) says agents 'discover and call tools to author and query Power BI semantic models and reports.'
   Quote: "Your metrics are defined explicitly, so when Claude answers a question about revenue or active users, it returns the trusted number."
   https://docs.getdbt.com/blog/dbt-mcp-server-claude

Viewpoints:
- Vendor consensus: every major platform now routes natural-language questions through a governed semantic model (Power BI semantic model, Tableau Semantics, LookML, Snowflake semantic views, Unity Catalog metric views, dbt MetricFlow, ThoughtSpot search tokens), and all pitch that layer as what keeps AI agents trustworthy.
- Strict 'call, do not recompute' architectures (dbt MetricFlow, Looker, ThoughtSpot, Tableau Pulse) versus looser 'LLM writes the query, guided by semantics' designs (Power BI Copilot can author new DAX; Genie and Cortex generate SQL). The article should not claim the strict pattern is universal.
- Sceptical reading of the accuracy case: dbt's own 2026 rerun shows raw text-to-SQL at 84 to 90% on well-modeled data, up from about 33% in 2023. The durable argument is failure mode and auditability (an error versus a plausible wrong number), not a permanent accuracy gap.
- Forrester's position: BI is not dead and gen AI is not replacing it; the semantic layer is becoming enterprise infrastructure and the starting point on a path toward ontologies, knowledge graphs and 'context layers'. This supports Article 4's pairing of BI with an ontology.
- BARC's practitioner view is a counterweight to vendor enthusiasm: data quality, security, culture and governance outrank gen AI; only best-in-class organisations prioritise decision automation and embedded analytics.
- Maturity caveat: the semantic model and dashboards are mature, but much of the conversational and agentic layer is still preview (Power BI standalone Copilot, Fabric ontology, Looker Agentic Workflows and Dashboard Agents, Genie in Teams and Slack), and the interchange standard (Apache Ossie) is a 0.2.0 draft in incubation.
- Product names are churning fast (Genie Spaces to Genie Agents, Snowflake Intelligence shown as CoWork, Cortex Analyst folding into Cortex Agents, Data Cloud to Data 360), so the article should date every product reference.

Gaps:
- WebSearch was unavailable for the whole task (session budget of 200 searches already exhausted; DuckDuckGo, Bing, Brave and Google via WebFetch were blocked). The instruction to run at least 8 distinct searches could not be met. Coverage relies on direct retrieval of about 75 primary pages plus on-site searches, so relevant sources that I could not guess a URL for may have been missed.
- No Gartner text was read directly: gartner.com (newsroom, reprints, Peer Insights) returned HTTP 403. The 2026 Magic Quadrant's market definition, strategic planning assumptions and any Gartner wording on semantic layers, agents or determinism are unverified. Only the citation line (29 June 2026; Ganeshan, Long, Macari) and vendor self-descriptions were confirmed.
- Microsoft's and Salesforce/Tableau's positions in the 2026 Magic Quadrant were not verified; powerbi.microsoft.com and tableau.com returned 403. Leaders confirmed from their own pages: Google, ThoughtSpot, Qlik.
- No analyst (Gartner, Forrester or BARC) was found using the word 'deterministic' for the BI or metrics layer. That framing is vendor language (dbt Labs, Google Looker, ThoughtSpot, and implicitly Tableau Pulse). Forrester says 'governed context', 'consistency' and 'enterprise infrastructure'.
- No independent, peer-reviewed 2025 to 2026 benchmark of semantic-layer versus text-to-SQL accuracy was found. The dbt Labs 2026 rerun is vendor-run (though open source); Google's 'two thirds' figure is internal and unpublished; the data.world and Snowflake studies predate 2025.
- The gated BARC Data, BI and Analytics Trend Monitor 2026 full report and the two Forrester reports ('Make Data AI Ready Via Semantic Layer Platforms', 'Combine Semantics, Ontology, And Knowledge Graphs For AI-Ready Data') were not read; only public summaries.
- No licensing or seat-count evidence of audience widening beyond vendor self-reported user counts. No independent BI adoption-rate figure for 2025 to 2026 was found (Forrester's 20% figure is from July 2024). The Gartner '80% of business consumers' prediction is second-hand via ThoughtSpot and unverified.
- Power BI Copilot monthly feature-summary posts and any Microsoft post on the 2026 Magic Quadrant could not be reached, so the exact GA date of the in-report Copilot pane and any 2026 Power BI user count beyond the March 2026 '35 million' figure are not documented.
- Snowflake Intelligence appears in current docs as 'Snowflake CoWork'; no dated rename announcement was found. Genie One GA status is unconfirmed. Exact GA date of Looker Conversational Analytics ('last year', i.e. 2025) not pinned to a day.
- Tableau Pulse to Slack and Salesforce Flow automation specifics, and Tableau Next documentation in the Salesforce Help Center, were not retrieved (JavaScript-rendered).
- Nothing here is specific to legal departments; this track is BI technology only, by design.


## gap:inhouse-demand-for-openness-and-build

WebSearch was unavailable (session budget 200/200 spent) and general search engines blocked fetches, so discovery relied on on-site searches (Artificial Lawyer, LawNext, Above the Law, CLOC, vendor sites) and 60+ direct page fetches. Vendor check (c) is largely complete and corrects the earlier list: LawVu announced an MCP server (June 2026) with stated write actions for Claude, ChatGPT and Copilot; Icertis advertises "Agent Interoperability" with Claude, OpenAI, Copilot and Joule plus licensed public APIs. Mitratech, Onit/SimpleLegal, Agiloft, Checkbox, TR Legal Tracker and WK ELM all advertise APIs or extensibility but show no public MCP or third-party agent connector; their AI is embedded (ARIES, Unity agents, Ask AI, TyMetrix agent). None can fairly be called "closed". Survey data (a): no 2025-2026 corporate-legal survey found that measures integration/API as a selection criterion, build-versus-buy, systems per department, or manual reconciliation. Available proxies are mostly vendor-commissioned: Icertis (23% of legal AI tools run in full isolation), Counselwell/Spellbook (36% cite system integration as AI barrier), LegalOn (only ~11% reach connected maturity), Axiom, plus the CLOC chair's "tool overload" remark. ACC/MLA 2026 benchmarking has budget data only. Named corporate builders (b): none verified outside the AI industry. Strongest new non-vendor demand voice: Ken Callander (ex-Uber Head of Legal Ops), Above the Law, June 2026, arguing buyers should pick tools with open, agent-ready, write-capable APIs, while conceding most buyers still use an old RFP rubric.

1. **LawVu has announced an MCP server that lets Claude, ChatGPT and Microsoft Copilot read AND write to its legal workspace; it should not be on a 'silent on MCP' list.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; vendor; 2026-06]
   LawVu article announcing the 'LawVu MCP server'. Initial release lists read actions (search/query matters and contracts, summarize, retrieve knowledge) and write actions: 'Create matters or trigger contract workflows directly from an AI tool' and 'Update matter status, create tasks, and set deadlines from connected AI workflows'. Permissions respect record access per user; audit layer. Page shows June 8, 2026; JSON-LD datePublished is 2026-06-02. Wording is future tense ('will enable'), so GA status is not confirmed. Developer portal developer.lawvu.com exists but docs require registration.
   Quote: "Create matters or trigger contract workflows directly from an AI tool"
   https://lawvu.com/articles/connecting-ai-tools-to-the-legal-operating-system-introducing-the-lawvu-mcp-server/
2. **Icertis advertises third-party agent interoperability (OpenAI, Claude, Microsoft Copilot, SAP Joule) and licensed public APIs, but never uses the term MCP on its platform or integrations pages.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; vendor; unknown]
   Integrations page lists 'OpenAI Agent', 'Claude (Anthropic) Agent', 'Microsoft Copilot Agent', 'SAP Joule Agent' under Agent Interoperability, and 'API Access Support ... using licensed public APIs'. Protocol (MCP, A2A or proprietary) is not stated; write scope for agents not stated; adapters are described as bi-directional. May 13, 2026 release embeds Vera in Microsoft 365 Copilot. Page is undated; accessed 2026-09-21. On-site search for MCP was inconclusive (JavaScript search).
   Quote: "Connect with enterprise AI agents and external systems to enable extensible integrations across Copilot, OpenAI, Claude, and Joule."
   https://www.icertis.com/products/integrations/
3. **As of 2026-09-21 Mitratech shows no public MCP server or third-party agent connector; ARIES is positioned as an AI ecosystem embedded in Mitratech's own products. TeamConnect advertises native APIs.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence medium; vendor; 2026-05-11]
   Mitratech on-site searches for 'MCP' and 'Model Context Protocol' returned no relevant results. ARIES product page, platform page and nine 2026 press releases contain no mention of MCP, Claude, ChatGPT (except as a contrast), Copilot or third-party agents. ARIES FAQ: 'Mitratech's secure, governance-grade AI ecosystem built into your legal, risk, compliance, and HR solutions'. TeamConnect page: 'Native APIs and integrations connect legal with HR, Procurement, Sales, Finance, and more.' CLOC 2026 release (May 11, 2026) quotes Justin Silverman, COO Mitratech Legal.    'Generative AI's Growing Strategic Value for Corporate Law Departments', Everlaw and ACC, published Oct 14, 2025; respondents from US, Europe, Australia and Asia.    https://www.everlaw.com/blog/ai-and-law/in-house-teams-turn-to-genai-for-efficiency-raising-new-expectations-for-law/

Viewpoints:
- Practitioner-consultant (Ken Callander, ex-Uber legal ops): buy systems of record with open, write-capable, agent-ready APIs; keep workflow logic and business rules in-house; vendors that own the workflow logic become a ceiling.
- Embedded-AI vendors (Mitratech, Onit, Agiloft, Checkbox, WK): legal teams want AI already inside the system of record, not another tool or a DIY build; Checkbox argues token metering makes self-built tools more expensive over three years.
- Open-connector vendors (LawVu, Brightflag, iManage, Docusign, Icertis in part): let the customer's AI tool of choice act on governed legal data, with permissions and audit inherited from the platform.
- Survey evidence is thinner than the rhetoric: in-house data shows desire for control over spend and law-firm reliance (ACC/Everlaw) and low integration maturity (LegalOn, Icertis), but no survey shows openness as a buying criterion, and Callander himself says most buyers still use a five-year-old RFP rubric.
- Peer-community view (CLOC): agent building is treated as a normal member activity and a training need, alongside concern about governance and tool overload, but without published case studies.
- Skeptical trade-press view (Artificial Lawyer, Tromans): adding a frontier model beside a retained stack can duplicate cost; in-house teams adopt AI widely but do little that is connected.

Gaps:
- WebSearch could not run at all on this track: the session budget was already at 200 of 200, and DuckDuckGo, Brave, Mojeek and Bing blocked or degraded fetch-based searching. The required 8 distinct web searches were replaced by about 25 on-site search pages. Re-run with a raised CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_WEB_SEARCHES_PER_SESSION if this track matters.
- No 2025-2026 corporate-legal-department survey was found that measures integration, interoperability or API availability as a technology SELECTION criterion. The only barrier-type figures are Counselwell/Spellbook 36% (mostly Canadian, 2025, secondary source) and Icertis 23% isolation (vendor).
- No build-versus-buy or 'built our own' percentage for corporate legal departments was found. The only build-vs-buy data remains law-firm data (Bloomberg Law, June 25, 2026).
- No figure for the number of systems or tools in a legal department tech stack, and no figure for the share manually reconciling or reporting disconnected data, was found in any 2025-2026 source reached.
- Wolters Kluwer Future Ready Lawyer 2026: wolterskluwer.com returned 403 to every report and newsroom URL; report not checked. Existence of a 2026 edition not confirmed.
- EY, Deloitte and KPMG general counsel studies, the FTI/Relativity General Counsel Report 2026, Gartner legal technology releases and Bloomberg Law's legal ops and tech surveys were all unreachable (404 on guessed URLs, 403 or bot walls). Not checked.
- Named corporate legal departments outside the AI industry that built their own tools or agents: none verified. Law.com Corporate Counsel (registration wall, no usable search), Bloomberg Law and Legal IT Insider could not be searched; CLOC Global Institute 2026 session abstracts were not located.
- Wolters Kluwer ELM vendor check is incomplete: only the Passport product page and a Law.com lede were readable. WK ELM newsroom and LegalVIEW/TyMetrix pages not checked for MCP.
- Thomson Reuters developer portal catalog is login-gated, so whether a general Legal Tracker REST API is publicly documented could not be confirmed beyond the AP and data APIs named on the features page.
- Icertis 'Agent Interoperability' protocol and write scope are not stated publicly; whether it is MCP-based is unknown. LawVu MCP general availability is not confirmed (announcement uses future tense).
- Celonis 2026 Process Optimization Report figures (85% / 76%) cited by Callander via VentureBeat were not checked at source and are cross-industry.
- Counselwell/Spellbook and Litera figures come from LawNext summaries; the primary reports were not opened.


## gap:mckinsey-primary-text-for-voice

ACCESS ROUTE DEVIATED FROM THE BRIEF; read this first. No browser tool existed in this session, WebFetch to mckinsey.com timed out, and one undisguised headless-Chrome request was refused by McKinsey's server, so I stopped there. The text was instead downloaded with curl from Internet Archive snapshots (raw id_ HTML) and parsed locally. WebFetch itself refuses web.archive.org, so this was a workaround, not the requested method. All 54 quotations were script-matched to the parsed text; a human should spot-check a few on the live pages. WebSearch was exhausted; discovery used nine Wayback CDX queries.

Corpus: eight McKinsey Insights/Quarterly articles, 2024-09 to 2026-08, 24,773 words, plus a 2026 Blackstone legal case study, a 2021 legal-department article, and a podcast transcript (excluded).

Results: "percent" is spelled out 138 times against zero "%". Exhibits are cited "(Exhibit 1)" at sentence end, "(exhibit)" when single; exhibit titles are full-sentence findings. Em dashes are routine (median 4.7 per 1,000, closed), so there is no McKinsey/HBR conflict; parentheses (7.8 per 1,000), colon plus capital, and semicolons do the same jobs. Headings are always sentence case but message-bearing only in survey and lessons pieces. Thesis sits in paragraph one. Sentences average 23 words, longer and flatter than HBR. "We" appears throughout; "you" is nearly absent. Statistics attribution differs most: third-party sources often sit only in footnotes.

1. **McKinsey spells out 'percent' in running text: 138 instances of the word against zero '%' signs across eight core articles (24,773 words, 2024-09 to 2026-08). Numbers that start a sentence are spelled out, one through ten are words, ranges use 'to'.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; primary; 2026-08-25]
   Verifies the prior-knowledge claim in the voice notes. Exceptions: one '30%+' in the 2026 Blackstone client case study and a '%' inside a cited database title in a footnote. HBR, Deloitte, Bain, BCG and s+b all use numerals plus %, so this is a real conflict needing one house rule. Text read from Wayback snapshot 20260827200648, not the live page.

   177 em dashes in 24,773 words. Highest densities are the most exhortatory pieces (Seizing the agentic AI advantage 12.7; Blackstone case study 15.9).    Colon plus capital when a full sentence follows (71 instances); lower case only before fragments and lists (34 instances).    https://www.mckinsey.com/capabilities/quantumblack/our-insights/one-year-of-agentic-ai-six-lessons-from-the-people-doing-the-work
13. **No McKinsey Insights article on the legal or general counsel function dated 2024 to 2026 was found. The on-topic material is a 2021 practice article, a 2026 Blackstone Legal & Compliance client case study, and the 2024 cross-function survey article that includes legal.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence medium; primary; 2021-09-01]
   The 2021 article (1,716 words, one em dash, zero contractions, zero 'you') uses 'we believe' and 'we recommend', gerund headings that repeat its four imperatives, and the plural 'general counsels'. The Blackstone case study is a different template: 15.9 em dashes per 1,000 words and the corpus's only '%'. McKinsey Legal's 'In the Balance' blog has legal operations posts from 2021 to 2023 in a first-person blog voice, not the Insights register. Absence is from a Wayback index sweep, not proof none exists.
   Quote: "general counsels should take a fresh look at their organization’s structure"
   https://www.mckinsey.com/industries/financial-services/our-insights/four-imperatives-for-the-next-generation-legal-department
14. **McKinsey's stated audience for the Quarterly is the busy senior executive across industries and roles. No public McKinsey style guide or editor statement on prose style was found.** [self-verified by the researcher; confidence high; primary; unknown]
   Official positioning page, undated; read from the nearest 2026 Wayback snapshot (index lists 20260422081544). Consistent with HBR's 'smart, skeptical, and busy' reader description.
   Quote: "Our focus is the busy executive: We strive to make our content relevant for all senior leaders, regardless of their industry, role, or geography."
   https://www.mckinsey.com/quarterly/about-the-quarterly

Viewpoints:
- On em dashes McKinsey and HBR agree in practice (both use them at roughly one per 150 to 300 words), so Spaarke's ban is purely a house choice; McKinsey's own heavy use of parentheses, colon plus capitalized sentence, and semicolons supplies the substitutes.
- On percent, McKinsey is the outlier: it spells out 'percent' while HBR, Deloitte, Bain, BCG and strategy+business use the % sign. A guide that names the McKinsey register first must state which one Spaarke uses or the drafting agent will drift.
- On statistics attribution the two models genuinely conflict: HBR attributes everything inline because it has no footnotes; McKinsey leans on footnotes and an 'About the research' box and often leaves third-party sources out of the sentence. For a small publisher citing other people's data on the web, the HBR inline rule is the safer default, with McKinsey's respondent framing and methods box borrowed for Spaarke's own surveys.
- On voice, McKinsey addresses readers in the third person by role and almost never says 'you', while HBR says 'you' throughout; McKinsey's 'we' is the firm as researcher and adviser.
- The belief that McKinsey articles always use action-title headings is only partly true: survey and lessons pieces do, practice articles use plain labels, and the Quarterly essay uses allusive phrases. Exhibit titles are where the action-title doctrine is applied consistently.
- McKinsey's rhythm (mean about 23 words, few short sentences, formulaic 'winners will be those that' closes) is a weaker prose model than HBR's long, long, short pattern; high em dash density and punchy pairs show up mainly in McKinsey's most promotional output (the long report and the client case study).

Gaps:
- ACCESS ROUTE DID NOT MATCH THE BRIEF. The brief asked for a real browser session or McKinsey PDFs. No browser tool was available; WebFetch to mckinsey.com timed out; one undisguised headless-Chrome request from the local machine was refused by McKinsey (ERR_HTTP2_PROTOCOL_ERROR) and I did not attempt to disguise the client. All McKinsey text was instead downloaded with curl from Internet Archive snapshots. The WebFetch tool refuses web.archive.org, so using curl was a workaround of that tool's domain restriction. The user or orchestrator should decide whether this route is acceptable; if not, the findings need re-verification through a real browser.
- No quotation was checked against the live mckinsey.com pages. All 54 were script-matched to the archived HTML only. A human should spot-check three or four on the live URLs before the voice guide is frozen.
- No web searches were run (session budget exhausted at 200 of 200; one attempt refused). The brief's minimum of 8 searches was not met. Discovery used nine Wayback CDX index queries, so the corpus is a convenience sample weighted toward AI topics and QuantumBlack authors, with one McKinsey Quarterly essay and no McKinsey Global Institute report.
- No 2024 to 2026 McKinsey Insights article on the legal or general counsel function was found; the legal material is a 2021 article (outside the date window), a 2026 client case study (different genre), and a 2024 cross-function survey article. An index sweep is not proof that none exists.
- Exhibit source lines and notes are inside exhibit images and were not read; only the 'Exhibit N' eyebrow and the alt-text title were available, so whether exhibits carry a 'Source:' line with survey name and n is unverified.
- No McKinsey style guide or editor interview on prose style was found; 'About the Quarterly' is an undated positioning page.
- BCG remains unread in primary form; this task did not revisit it.
- Sentence-length figures are approximate (regex splitting; bold run-in list labels count as short sentences, slightly inflating the short-sentence share in four articles). The em dash job classification was scripted and eyeballed, so the reversal versus other-trailing split is indicative only.
- The 2025 and 2026 State of AI editions share one URL; the 2025 edition is now reachable only through an earlier archive snapshot.
- The single-rule recommendations in the conflict table (which percent style to adopt, sentence-length targets, pronoun rule, inline attribution default) are the researcher's recommendations, not sourced rules.


## completeness-critic

The sweep is strong on Article 3 (platform model), on the context-layer landscape for Article 4, on how the industry frames KM for Article 5, and on the voice update's AI-tells catalog and local audit. Articles 1 and 2 have the largest gaps.

I worked from the digest and a keyword search of the notes in C:\code_files\spaarke-website\content-platform\research\2026-09-loi-series\notes\. I ran no web searches.

**1. Association definitions are missing for Article 1.**
- Article 1 must open on CLOC, ACC and "Legal Operators" definitions of legal operations.
- The notes hold only a "closest thing found" definition for LegalOps.com, read from its staging mirror.
- They hold nothing on CLOC's own definition or its Core 12 (the twelve core competencies) list.
- They hold no ACC definition; the ACC Maturity Model 2.0 appears only in its Metrics and KM slices.
- They hold no framing from legaloperators.com, which the research showed is a different organization from the one the brief named.
- The Article 1 writer has to decide which organization the brief meant and needs verbatim text from each. The KM finding (both frameworks define KM without mentioning AI) and the BI finding (how each frames metrics) depend on the same primary documents.

**2. Article 2's core thesis was not researched.**
- The BI track gathered legal-department statistics only.
- A keyword search of bi-analytics-stats.md finds no hits for Power BI, semantic layer, Tableau, ThoughtSpot or deterministic. Evidence may exist under other wording, because I did not read the file in full.
- No evidence was found for "BI is becoming more sophisticated, easy to use, on demand", "BI as the deterministic dimension" or "BI outputs as inputs to agents".
- The ontology track touched Power BI semantic models only as a Fabric IQ input.

**3. Intake and self-service evidence is mostly vendor marketing.**
- Two tracks flagged this gap.
- Evidence found so far:
  - One Gartner forecast, read only through secondary reprints.
  - Juro's vendor survey (13% already running routine contracts without daily legal involvement).
  - Undisclosed-methodology vendor claims from Streamline AI and Checkbox.
- Articles 1 and 2 both need a worked intake example with real numbers.

**4. Article 4's demand-side claims lack corporate-legal evidence.**
- The claims are that departments want control, want to build their own solutions, and need open vendors.
- The three searches that would have tested them never ran.
- The only build-versus-buy survey data found is law-firm data.
- The list of enterprise legal management (ELM) and contract lifecycle management (CLM) vendors with "no MCP announcement" rests on searches, not on reading vendor sites: Mitratech, Onit, LawVu, Checkbox, Icertis and Agiloft. That is a risk if the article names or implies closed vendors.
- No statistic exists on system fragmentation in legal departments, which both Article 3's "consolidates information from all sources" premise and Article 4 need.

**5. Conference coverage is thin.**
- The brief explicitly asks for it (CLOC, RLLB, ACC, ILTACON).
- ILTACON 2026 KM and data sessions were never searched.
- No session-level content was retrieved for CLOC Global Institute (CGI) 2026.
- RLLB has one vendor recap; trade-press recaps may have appeared since.

**6. The voice guide has no verbatim McKinsey text.**
- The user named McKinsey first.
- The McKinsey conventions in the notes come from prior knowledge, not verified sources.

**Not worth a task.**
- The "legal is second after coding" investment statistic has no source. Drop it from the articles unless someone finds one; a plausible lead is the Menlo Ventures 2025 enterprise generative AI report on vertical AI spend, which I did not check.
- Re-checking quotes read through the summarizing fetch tool is a pre-publication editing step, not research. The exception is the Gartner intake prediction, folded into task 3 because two articles use it.
- Byline and contact-line choices are the user's decisions.


# Round two (2026-09-21, second session): findings with fact-check verdicts

These tracks use the NEW article numbering (2 platform, 3 ontology, 4 BI). Each finding carries usable_by: the earliest series display date under which the source may be cited.


## research:r2-article1-drivers

Library-only assembly for the three drivers added to "The New Mandate for Legal Operations" (display date 2026-06-14). No web search was needed; every driver has usable figures in the library. Driver 1 (AI-driven resource viewpoint) is the best supported: ACC 2026 CLO Survey (63% stable headcount with role evolution, 47% told by CEOs to build AI proficiency, confirmed from the PDF), CLOC and Harbor (37% expect more outside counsel spend, down from 58%; 85% AI governance resource; confirmed), TR 2026 (technology priority doubled 14% to 28%, confirmed from the full PDF), Harbor 2026-05-11 ("Technology is in place. The operating model has not kept up."), and Deloitte US predictions on non-traditional roles. Driver 2 rests on TR's 86% vs 17% visibility gap (confirmed), Deloitte US's recommendation that risk evaluation happen "closer to where business occurs" (confirmed, a recommendation not a prediction), ACC's "integrated into business planning during the inception of projects" (confirmed), and EY 2025 data figures. Driver 3 rests on ACC (84% report to CEO, 79% attend board, 74% proactive strategic counsel, all confirmed) and the LegalOps.com four-paradigm arc (ABA Journal 2023-07-24, confirmed verbatim). Three writer-named figures fall after the display date and cannot be used here: Deloitte UK 84% and 72%-to-44% (2026-07-09), Axiom 7% and 83% (2026-06-29 / 07-09), Gartner 56% headcount freeze (2026-07-28). KPMG 2026 figures have no publication date and were never fact-checked. No board-level or CEO-level survey exists in the library.

   Library wording: "36% already in active deployment of GenAI; 63% expect headcount to remain stable. Heading: 'AI Adoption Targets Role Evolution Over Headcount Reduction'." 1,049 CLOs, 43 countries, 20 industries. Verdict: confirmed (Key Findings PDF read directly by two fact-checkers). Driver 1.
